F.G. has been partially supported by the MUR grant Dipartimento di Eccellenza 2023–2027 of Dipartimento di Matematica, Politecnico di Milano and is a member of the INdAM-GNAMPA group. We thank Ian Dryden, Karthik Bharath and Theodore Kypraios for fruitful discussions during the manuscript preparation.

A
Proof of the results regarding the mixed quantum Gaussian model

Let us consider two states

\rho(x),\rho(y)
in
\mathbf{GM}
corresponding to
x,y\in X
respectively. From equation (
15
) one can check that the states coincide if and only if
x_{0}=y_{0}
and there exist
\theta_{0},\dots,\theta_{p-1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\ket{\zeta_m(y_0^\perp)}=e^{\frac{2\pi\theta_{m}i}{p}}\ket{\zeta_m(x_0^\perp)}
for
m=0,\dots,p-1
; notice that
\theta_{m}
are only determined modulo
p
. Using the explicit expression for
\ket{\zeta_m(x_0^\perp)}
and
\ket{\zeta_m(y_0^\perp)}
, we can rewrite the previous condition as

\begin{split}&e^{-\|y_{0}^{\perp}\|^{2}/2}\left(\delta_{0}(m)\ket{\Omega_0^\perp}+\sum_{l\geq 1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{l!}}\sum_{m_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus m_{l}=m}R_{m_{1}}\ket{y_0^\perp}\otimes\cdots\otimes R_{m_{l}}\ket{y_0^\perp}\right)=\\
&e^{\frac{2\pi\theta_{m}i}{p}}e^{-\|x_{0}^{\perp}\|^{2}/2}\left(\delta_{0}(m)\ket{\Omega_0^\perp}+\sum_{l\geq 1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{l!}}\sum_{m_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus m_{l}=m}R_{m_{1}}\ket{x_0^\perp}\otimes\cdots\otimes R_{m_{l}}\ket{x_0^\perp}\right),\end{split}

which, in turn, is equivalent to

e^{-\|y_{0}^{\perp}\|^{2}/2}=e^{\frac{2\pi\theta_{0}i}{p}}e^{-\|x_{0}^{\perp}\|^{2}/2}

(75)

and for every
l\geq 1
,
m=0,\dots,p-1
,
m_{1}\oplus\cdots m_{l}=m

e^{-\|y_{0}^{\perp}\|^{2}/2}R_{m_{1}}\ket{y_0^\perp}\otimes\cdots\otimes R_{m_{l}}\ket{y_0^\perp}=e^{-\|x_{0}^{\perp}\|^{2}/2}e^{\frac{2\pi\theta_{m}i}{2}}R_{m_{1}}\ket{x_0^\perp}\otimes\cdots\otimes R_{m_{l}}\ket{x_0^\perp}.

(76)

Notice that Eq. (
75
) implies that
\theta_{0}\equiv_{p}0
and
\|y_{0}^{\perp}\|=\|x_{0}^{\perp}\|
. Moreover, if we choose
l=1
in Eq. (
76
), we obtain

R_{m}\ket{y_0^\perp}=e^{2\pi\theta_{m}i}R_{m}\ket{x_0^\perp},\quad m=1,\dots,p-1.

Choosing
l=m=1,\dots,p-1
and
m_{1}=\dots=m_{m}=1
, one gets

m\theta_{1}\equiv_{p}\theta_{m},\quad p\theta_{1}\equiv_{p}0,

which implies that

\theta_{1}\equiv_{p}k
for some
k=0,\dots,p-1
and
\theta_{m}\equiv_{p}mk
. Recalling that
U^{k}\cdot R_{m}\ket{x_0^\perp}=e^{\frac{2mk\pi i}{p}}R_{m}\ket{x_0^\perp}
for every
k,m=0,\dots,p-1
, one obtains the statement.

∎

From the splitting
{\cal V}_{0}^{\perp}=\bigoplus_{m=1}^{p-1}{\cal V}_{m}
, for every
n\geq 1
, we can write

({\cal V}_{0}^{\perp})^{\otimes_{s}n}=\bigoplus_{m=0}^{p-1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{n,m}({\cal V}_{0}^{\perp}),\quad{\mathfrak{H}}_{n,m}({\cal V}_{0}^{\perp})={\rm Symm}\left[\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\dots,m_{n}=0,\dots,p-1\\
m_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus m_{n}=m\end{subarray}}{\cal V}_{m_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\cal V}_{m_{n}}\right].

For
n=0
, we denote
{\mathfrak{H}}_{0,0}({\cal V}_{0}^{\perp})=\mathbb{C}\ket{\Omega_0^\perp}
and
{\cal H}_{0,m}({\cal V}_{0}^{\perp})=\{0\}
for
m=1,\dots,p-1
.
Let us define
Q_{m}
as the orthogonal projection onto

{\mathfrak{V}}_{m}:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}{\mathfrak{H}}_{n,m}({\cal V}_{0}^{\perp})=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\,\left[\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\dots,m_{n}=0,\dots,p-1\\
m_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus m_{n}=m\end{subarray}}{\cal V}_{m_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\cal V}_{m_{n}}.\right]

Note that
\Gamma(U)=\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\gamma^{m}\mathbf{1}\otimes Q_{m}
: indeed, this follows from the fact that

\Gamma(U)_{|{\cal V}_{m_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\cal V}_{m_{n}}}=U^{\otimes n}_{|{\cal V}_{m_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\cal V}_{m_{n}}}=\gamma^{m_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus m_{n}}\mathbf{1}_{|{\cal V}_{m_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\cal V}_{m_{n}}}.

Therefore, for every
\ket{x}=\ket{x_0}+\ket{x_0^\perp}
such that
\ket{x_0}\in{\cal V}_{0}
and
\ket{x_0^\perp}\in{\cal V}_{0}^{\perp}
, we can write

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\alpha_{U*}^{k}(W(x)|\Omega\rangle\langle\Omega|W(x)^{*})\\
&=W(x_{0})|\Omega_{0}\rangle\langle\Omega_{0}|W(x_{0})^{*}\otimes\sum_{m,m^{\prime}=0}^{p-1}\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\gamma^{k(m^{\prime}-m)}\right)}_{=\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}}Q_{m^{\prime}}W(x_{0}^{\perp})|\Omega_{0}^{\perp}\rangle\langle\Omega_{0}^{\perp}|W(x_{0}^{\perp})^{*}Q_{m}\\
&=W(x_{0})|\Omega_{0}\rangle\langle\Omega_{0}|W(x_{0})^{*}\otimes\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}Q_{m}W(x_{0}^{\perp})|\Omega_{0}^{\perp}\rangle\langle\Omega_{0}^{\perp}|W(x_{0}^{\perp})^{*}Q_{m}\\
&=W(x_{0})|\Omega_{0}\rangle\langle\Omega_{0}|W(x_{0})^{*}\otimes\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\ket{\zeta_m(x_0^{\perp})}\bra{\zeta_m(x_0^{\perp})},\end{split}

where

\begin{split}&\ket{\zeta_m(x_0^{\perp})}=Q_{m}W(x_{0}^{\perp})|\Omega_{0}^{\perp}\rangle\\
&=e^{-\|x_{0}^{\perp}\|^{2}/2}\left(\delta_{0}(m)\ket{\Omega_0^\perp}+\sum_{l\geq 1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{l!}}\sum_{m_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus m_{l}=m}R_{m_{1}}\ket{x_0^\perp}\otimes\cdots\otimes R_{m_{l}}\ket{x_0^\perp}\right).\end{split}

In the last equation we applied formula (
2
) for the action of Weyl operators.

∎

B
Proofs of lemmas on convergence of statistical models

In this section we improve on Lemma 5 in
[
86
]
showing that under suitable assumptions one can upgrade weak convergence to strong convergence in pure state models even when the parameter space is not compact. Before proceeding, we recall the following well known fact; since we could not find any reference, we report the short proof for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma 8
.

Let
A
and
B
be two positive semidefinite
d\times d
matrices with complex entries. The following holds true:

\|\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B}\|_{\infty}\leq\|A-B\|_{\infty}^{1/2}.

Let
x
be any normalized eigenvector at which
\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B}
attains the norm, i.e.

(\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B})x=\lambda x\text{ with }|\lambda|=\|\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B}\|_{\infty}.

Notice that
A-B=\sqrt{A}(\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B})+(\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B})\sqrt{B}
, therefore

\langle x,(A-B)x\rangle=\langle x,\sqrt{A}(\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B})x\rangle+\langle x(\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B})\sqrt{B}x\rangle=\lambda\langle x(\sqrt{A}+\sqrt{B})x\rangle.

Let us consider

\|\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B}\|_{\infty}^{2}=(\langle x,(\sqrt{A}-\sqrt{B})x\rangle)^{2}\leq|\lambda|\langle x,(\sqrt{A}+\sqrt{B})x\rangle=|\langle x,(A-B)x\rangle|\leq\|A-B\|_{\infty}.

∎

Lemma 9
(Weak to strong convergence for rank one operators).

Let
\{r_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of positive numbers such that
r_{n}\uparrow+\infty
,
C
a positive constant,
X
be a normed space of finite dimension
d
,
A
a finite set. Moreover, let us consider the sequence of collections of rank-one operators given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(n):=\{\rho_{n}(x):=\ket{v_n(x,a)}\bra{v_n(x,a)}\}_{\{(x,a)\in{\cal U}(n)\times A\}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}^{\infty}:=\{\rho(x):=\ket{v(x,a)}\bra{v(x,a)}\}_{\{(x,a)\in X\times A\}},

where
{\cal U}(n):=B_{Cr_{n}}(0)\subseteq H
is the ball centered in
0
with radius
Cr_{n}
and we assume that
\|v_{n}(x,a)\|,\|v(x,a)\|\leq 1
. We will use
\mathbf{Q}^{\infty}(n)
to denote the model
\mathbf{Q}^{\infty}
restricted to
{\cal U}(n)\times A
.

Let us assume that the following hypotheses hold true:

1.

(Hölder parametrization)
there exist positive constants
C_{1},C_{2},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
such that for every
x,y\in X
and
a\in A

\displaystyle\|v(x,a)\|^{2}\|v(y,a)\|^{2}-|\langle v(x,a),v(y,a)\rangle|^{2}\leq C_{1}\|x-y\|^{\alpha_{1}},

\displaystyle(\|v(x,a)\|^{2}-\|v(y,a)\|^{2})^{2}\leq C_{2}\|x-y\|^{\alpha_{2}}

2.

(Fast enough uniform weak convergence)
let

f(n):=\sup_{(x,a),(y,b)\in{\cal U}(n)\times A}|\langle v_{n}(x,a),v_{n}(y,b)\rangle-\langle v(x,a),v(y,b)\rangle|;

assume that

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}r_{n}^{d}f(n)=0.

Notice that, in this case, we can find a sequence of positive numbers
\{\delta_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
and
r_{n}^{d}f(n)=o(\delta_{n}^{d}).

Then

\Delta(\mathbf{Q}(n),\mathbf{Q}^{\infty}(n))=O\left(\delta_{n}^{\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}}+f(n)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\frac{r_{n}}{\delta_{n}}\right)^{\frac{d}{4}}f(n)^{\frac{1}{4}}\right)

and, therefore,

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\Delta(\mathbf{Q}(n),\mathbf{Q}^{\infty}(n))=0.

Note that it is always possible to find a finite set
I_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k(n)}\}\subseteq{\cal U}(n)
with
k(n)\lesssim(r_{n}/\delta_{n})^{d}
such that for every
x\in{\cal U}(n)
, there exists
x_{i}\in I_{n}
with
\|x-x_{i}\|\leq\delta_{n}
. This implies that the corresponding operators are close as well: using item
1
. one has that for
n
big enough the following holds true:

\begin{split}\|\rho(x,a)-\rho(x_{i},a)\|_{1}&=\sqrt{(\|v(x,a)\|^{2}-\|v(x_{i},a)\|^{2})^{2}+}\\
&\overline{+4(\|v(x,a)\|^{2}\|v(x_{i},a)\|^{2}-|\langle v(x,a),v(x_{i},a)\rangle|^{2})}\leq\widetilde{C}\delta_{n}^{\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}/2}.\end{split}

(77)

Moreover, note that

\begin{split}\|\rho_{n}(x,a)-\rho_{n}(x_{i},a)\|_{1}&=\|\rho(x,a)-\rho(x_{i},a)\|_{1}\quad(I)\\
&+\|\rho_{n}(x,a)-\rho_{n}(x_{i},a)\|_{1}-\|\rho(x,a)-\rho(x_{i},a)\|_{1}\quad(II).\end{split}

Eq. (
77
) implies that
(I)\leq\widetilde{C}\delta_{n}^{\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}/2}
for
n
big enough. Using that
x\mapsto\sqrt{x}
is Hölder
1/2
and item (
2
), one has that

\begin{split}&|\|\rho_{n}(x,a)-\rho_{n}(x_{i},a)\|_{1}-\|\rho(x,a)-\rho(x_{i},a)\|_{1}|\leq\\
&\sqrt{|(\|v(x,a)\|^{2}-\|v(x_{i},a)\|^{2})^{2}-(\|v_{n}(x,a)\|^{2}-\|v_{n}(x_{i},a)\|^{2})^{2}+}\\
&\overline{+4(\|v(x,a)\|^{2}\|v(x_{i},a)\|^{2}-\|v_{n}(x,a)\|^{2}\|v_{n}(x_{i},a)\|^{2})+}\\
&\overline{+4(|\langle v_{n}(x,a),v_{n}(x_{i},a)\rangle|^{2})-|\langle v(x,a),v(x_{i},a)\rangle|^{2})|}=O\left(f(n)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\end{split}

uniformly in
(x,a)\in{\cal U}(n)\times A
. Let us consider the Gram matrices of size
|A|\cdot k(n)

(G_{n}):=\langle v_{n}(x_{i},a),v_{n}(x_{j},b)\rangle,\quad(G^{\infty}_{n}):=\langle v(x_{i},a),v(x_{j},b)\rangle.

Notice that

\|G_{n}-G^{\infty}_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq|A|k(n)f(n)\lesssim\left(\frac{r_{n}}{\delta_{n}}\right)^{d}f(n)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow+\infty]{}0.

(78)

Let
\{e_{i,a}\}
be the canonical basis of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}:=\mathbb{C}^{|A|k(n)}
; we can define the following mappings:

v_{n}(x_{i},a)\mapsto\sqrt{G_{n}}e_{i,a}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{n},\quad v(x_{i},a)\mapsto\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}e_{i,a}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}

and extend them by linearity to two isometries denoted by
V_{n}
and, respectively,
V_{n}^{\infty}
, on

{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}:={\rm span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{v_{n}(x_{i},a):(x_{i},a)\in I_{n}\times A\}\text{ and }{\mathfrak{h}}^{\infty}_{n}:={\rm span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{v(x_{i},a):(x_{i},a)\in I_{n}\times A\}.

Let us consider the following Hilbert spaces:

\overline{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{n}:=\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{v_{n}(x,a):(x,a)\in{\cal U}(n)\times A\}},\quad\overline{{\mathfrak{h}}}^{\infty}_{n}:=\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{v(x,a):(x,a)\in{\cal U}(n)\times A\}}.

At the cost of possibly enlarging the dimension of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}
, we can consider two arbitrary extensions of
V_{n}
and
V_{n}^{\infty}
to
\overline{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{n}
and
\overline{{\mathfrak{h}}}^{\infty}_{n}
, respectively; with an abuse of notation we will still denote them by
V_{n}
and
V_{n}^{\infty}
. Let us introduce the following quantum channels:

A_{n}(\cdot):=V_{n}^{\infty*}V_{n}\cdot V_{n}^{*}V_{n}^{\infty},\quad B_{n}(\cdot):=V_{n}^{*}V^{\infty}_{n}\cdot V_{n}^{\infty*}V_{n}.

Let us consider any
x_{i}\in I_{n}
such that
\|x-x_{i}\|\leq\delta_{n}
; one has

\begin{split}\|A_{n}(\rho_{n}(x_{i},a))-\rho(x_{i},a)\|_{1}&=\|V_{n}^{\infty*}V_{n}\rho_{n}(x_{i},a)V_{n}^{*}V_{n}^{\infty}-\rho(x_{i},a)\|_{1}\\
&\leq\|V_{n}\rho_{n}(x_{i},a)V_{n}^{*}-V_{n}^{\infty}\rho(x_{i},a)V_{n}^{\infty*}\|_{1}\\
&=\left\|\ket{\sqrt{G_n}e_{i,a}}\bra{\sqrt{G_n}e_{i,a}}-\ket{\sqrt{G^\infty_n}e_{i,a}}\bra{\sqrt{G^\infty_n}e_{i,a}}\right\|_{1}\\
&=\sqrt{(\|\sqrt{G_{n}}e_{i,a}\|^{2}-\|\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}e_{i,a}\|^{2})^{2}+}\\
&\overline{+4\left(\|\sqrt{G_{n}}e_{i,a}\|^{2}\|\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}e_{i,a}\|^{2}-\left|\langle\sqrt{G_{n}}e_{i,a},\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}e_{i,a}\rangle\right|^{2}\right)}.\end{split}

Note that

(\|\sqrt{G_{n}}e_{i,a}\|^{2}-\|\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}e_{i,a}\|^{2})^{2}=(\langle e_{i,a},(G_{n}-G_{n}^{\infty})e_{i,a}\rangle)^{2}\leq\|G_{n}-G_{n}^{\infty}\|_{\infty}^{2}

and

\begin{split}&\|\sqrt{G_{n}}e_{i,a}\|^{2}\|\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}e_{i,a}\|^{2}-\left|\langle\sqrt{G_{n}}e_{i,a},\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}e_{i,a}\rangle\right|^{2}\\
&=\|\sqrt{G_{n}^{\infty}}e_{i,a}\|^{2}\langle\sqrt{G_{n}}e_{i,a},(\sqrt{G_{n}}-\sqrt{G_{n}^{\infty}})e_{i,a}\rangle\\
&+\langle\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}e_{i,a},(\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}-\sqrt{G_{n}})e_{i,a}\rangle\langle\sqrt{G_{n}}e_{i,a},\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}e_{i,a}\rangle\\
&\leq 2\|\sqrt{G^{\infty}_{n}}-\sqrt{G_{n}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|G^{\infty}_{n}-G_{n}\|^{1/2}_{\infty}.\end{split}

In the last inequality we used Lemma
8
. We obtained that

\max_{(x_{i},a)\in I_{n}\times A}\|A_{n}(\rho_{n}(x_{i},a))-\rho(x_{i},a)\|_{1}=O\left(\left(\frac{r_{n}}{\delta_{n}}\right)^{\frac{d}{4}}f(n)^{\frac{1}{4}}\right).

Analogously one can show that

\max_{(x_{i},a)\in I_{n}\times A}\|\rho_{n}(x_{i},a)-B_{n}(\rho(x_{i},a))\|_{1}=O\left(\left(\frac{r_{n}}{\delta_{n}}\right)^{\frac{d}{4}}f(n)^{\frac{1}{4}}\right).

We can finish our proof: let us consider
x\in{\cal U}(n)
,
a\in A
and
x_{i}\in I_{n}
such that
\|x-x_{i}\|\leq\delta_{n}
, then for
n
big enough one has

\begin{split}\|A_{n}(\rho_{n}(x,a))-\rho(x,a)\|_{1}&\leq\|A_{n}(\rho_{n}(x,a)-\rho_{n}(x_{i},a))\|_{1}+\|A_{n}(\rho_{n}(x_{i},a))-\rho(x_{i},a)\|_{1}\\
&+\|\rho_{n}(x_{i},a)-\rho(x,a)\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{C}\delta^{\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}/2}_{n}+O\left(\left(\frac{r_{n}}{\delta_{n}}\right)^{\frac{d}{4}}f(n)^{\frac{1}{4}}\right).\end{split}

We can repeat the same steps in order to get the statement for
\|\rho_{n}(x,a)-B_{n}(\rho(x,a))\|_{1}
as well.
∎

If we deal with statistical models, the statement of the previous Lemma becomes that of Lemma
2
in the main text.

Lemma 3.12 in
[
83
]
ensures that for every
n
there exist quantum channels
{\cal T}_{n}
and
{\cal S}_{n}
such that
\Delta(\mathbf{P}(n),\mathbf{P}^{\infty}(n))
is the maximum between

\sup_{(x,a)\in{\cal U}(n)\times A}\|{\cal T}_{n}(\ket{v_n(x,a)}\bra{v_n(x,a)})-\ket{v(x,a)}\bra{v(x,a)}\|_{1}

and

\sup_{(x,a)\in{\cal U}(n)\times A}\|\ket{v_n(x,a)}\bra{v_n(x,a)}-{\cal S}_{n}(\ket{v(x,a)}\bra{v(x,a)})\|_{1}.

Observe that for every
x\in{\cal U}(n)
one has

\begin{split}&\|{\cal T}_{n}(\rho_{n}(x))-\rho(x)\|_{1}=\left\|\sum_{a\in A}{\cal T}_{n}(\ket{v_n(x,a)}\bra{v_n(x,a)})-\ket{v(x,a)}\bra{v(x,a)}\right\|\leq\\
&\sum_{a\in A}\left\|{\cal T}_{n}(\ket{v_n(x,a)}\bra{v_n(x,a)})-\ket{v(x,a)}\bra{v(x,a)}\right\|\leq|A|\Delta(\mathbf{P}(n),\mathbf{P}^{\infty}(n)).\end{split}

We used linearity of the quantum channel and triangular inequality. Analogously, one obtains

\|\rho_{n}(x)-{\cal S}_{n}(\rho(x))\|_{1}\leq|A|\Delta(\mathbf{P}(n),\mathbf{P}^{\infty}(n)).

Therefore,

\Delta(\mathbf{Q}(n),\mathbf{Q}^{\infty}(n))\leq|A|\Delta(\mathbf{P}(n),\mathbf{P}^{\infty}(n))
and the statement follows immediately.
∎

C
Proof of the limit theorems for fluctuation observables

First, notice that the convergence of
\mathbb{P}(\overline{Q}_{n},\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho))
follows from the Central Limit Theorem for fluctuations operators derived below, by a simple application of Slutsky’s theorem. Let us now prove the Central Limit Theorem.

1. Reduction to an initial state supported only on one
{\cal H}_{a}
.
Notice that

\begin{split}\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho)&=\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in I_{n}}{\rm Tr}(K_{\mathbf{i}}\rho K_{\mathbf{j}}^{*})\ket{\mathbf{i}}\bra{\mathbf{j}}=\sum_{a,b=0}^{p-1}\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in I_{n}}{\rm Tr}(K_{\mathbf{i}}P_{a}\rho P_{b}K_{\mathbf{j}}^{*})\ket{\mathbf{i}}\bra{\mathbf{j}}\\
&=\sum_{a,b=0}^{p-1}\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in I_{n}}{\rm Tr}(P_{a\oplus n}K_{\mathbf{i}}P_{a}\rho P_{b}K_{\mathbf{j}}^{*}P_{b\oplus n})\ket{\mathbf{i}}\bra{\mathbf{j}}=\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in I_{n}}{\rm Tr}(K_{\mathbf{i}}P_{a}\rho P_{a}K_{\mathbf{j}}^{*})\ket{\mathbf{i}}\bra{\mathbf{j}}\\
&=\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}{\rm Tr}(\rho P_{a})\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho_{a}),\end{split}

where
\rho_{a}:=(P_{a}\rho P_{a})/{\rm Tr}(\rho P_{a})
(we interpret
\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho_{a})
as
0
if
{\rm Tr}(\rho P_{a})=0
). Therefore, we can reduce to prove the theorem for initial states which are supported in the range of only one
P_{a}
.

2. Reduction to a “coarser” output chain.
We recall that
Q
acts on a finite number of output units, let us say
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
; moreover, without loss of generality, we can assume that
{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n)Q)=0
(we can always reduce to this case centering
Q
). Let
M
be the smallest multiple of
p
which is bigger or equal than
k
and let us define
m=M/p
,
\tau_{l}
as the operator that sends any observable
X
acting on the first
k
output units into
X^{(l+1)}
, which acts on the output units
\{l+1,\dots,l+k\}
, and
\widetilde{\tau}_{l}=\tau_{lM}
for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

With a slight abuse of notation, for every
k,n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
k<n
, we will often make use of the identification
L^{\infty}({\cal K}^{\otimes k})\simeq L^{\infty}({\cal K}^{\otimes k})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}^{\otimes(n-k)}\subset L^{\infty}({\cal K}^{\otimes n})
; for instance, we will use the notation
{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V}(n,\rho)Q)
to denote
{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V}(n,\rho)Q\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}^{\otimes(n-k)})
.

We need to introduce the following sequence of operators:

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{n}(\widetilde{Q})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\lfloor(n-k)/M\rfloor^{1/2}}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor(n-k)/M\rfloor-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{l}(\widetilde{Q})\in L^{\infty}({\cal K}^{\otimes n}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{M^{1/2}}\sum_{l=0}^{M-1}\tau_{l}(Q)\in L^{\infty}({\cal K}^{\otimes(M+k)})\subseteq L^{\infty}({\cal K}^{\otimes 2M}).

For every
n
, let us use the notation
f(n)=\lfloor(n-k)/M\rfloor
and
r(n)=n-k+1-M\lfloor(n-k)/M\rfloor
; notice that
0\leq r(n)<M
and, if
n\rightarrow+\infty
, then
f(n)\rightarrow+\infty
as well. One has the following estimates:

\begin{split}\|F_{n}(Q)-\widetilde{F}_{n}(\widetilde{Q})\|_{\infty}&\leq\left|\frac{1}{(f(n)M+r(n))^{1/2}}-\frac{1}{(f(n)M)^{1/2}}\right|\left\|\sum_{l=0}^{f(n)M-1}\tau_{l}(Q)\right\|_{\infty}\\
&+\frac{1}{(n-k+1)^{1/2}}\left\|\sum_{l=f(n)M}^{n-k}\tau_{l}(Q)\right\|_{\infty}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{(f(n)M)^{1/2}}\underbrace{\left|\left(1+\frac{r(n)}{f(n)M}\right)^{-1/2}-1\right|}_{=O((f(n)M)^{-1})}f(n)M\|Q\|_{\infty}\\
&+\frac{1}{(n-k+1)^{1/2}}r(n)\|Q\|_{\infty}=O(n^{-1/2}).\end{split}

Therefore, for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
one has

\|e^{i\alpha F_{n}(Q)}-e^{i\alpha\widetilde{F}_{n}(\widetilde{Q})}\|_{\infty}=\left\|i\alpha\int_{0}^{1}e^{i\alpha s\widetilde{F}_{n}(\widetilde{Q})}(F_{n}(Q)-\widetilde{F}_{n}(\widetilde{Q}))e^{i(1-s)\alpha F_{n}(Q)}ds\right\|_{\infty}=O(n^{-1/2})

and, consequently,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left|{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho)e^{i\alpha F_{n}(Q)})-{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho)e^{i\alpha\widetilde{F}_{n}(\widetilde{Q})})\right|=0.

Therefore we can restrict to study the limit of
{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho)e^{i\alpha\widetilde{F}_{n}(\widetilde{Q})})
. Note that
\widetilde{F}_{n}(\widetilde{Q})
only changes when
n-k
is a multiple of
M
and, hence,
p
. Therefore,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho)e^{i\alpha\widetilde{F}_{n}(\widetilde{Q})})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(nM+k,\rho)e^{i\alpha\widetilde{F}_{nM+k}(\widetilde{Q})}).

Notice that
\widetilde{F}_{nM+k}(\widetilde{Q})
is a sequence of fluctuation operators obtained from a two site local operator
\widetilde{Q}\in L^{\infty}(\widetilde{{\cal K}}^{\otimes 2})
, where each new output unit is obtained grouping
M
original output units and, therefore, is described by the Hilbert space
\widetilde{{\cal K}}:={\cal K}^{\otimes M}
.

3. Reduction to stationarity.
Let us pick
j<n
,
j,n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, then

\begin{split}\left\|\widetilde{F}_{nM+k}(\widetilde{Q})-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\left(\widetilde{F}_{(n-j)M+k}(\widetilde{Q})\right)\right\|_{\infty}&\leq\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}\left\|\sum_{l=0}^{j-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{l}(\widetilde{Q})\right\|_{\infty}\\
&+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n-j}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\left\|\sum_{l=j}^{n-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{l}(\widetilde{Q})\right\|_{\infty}\\
&=O(n^{-1/2}).\end{split}

Note that

{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(nM+k,\rho)e^{i\alpha\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\left(\widetilde{F}_{(n-j)M}(\widetilde{Q})\right)}\right)={\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}((n-j)M+k,{\cal T}_{*}^{Mj}(\rho))e^{i\alpha\widetilde{F}_{(n-j)M}(\widetilde{Q})}\right)

and, since
M
is a multiple of
p
and
\rho
is supported in the range of
P_{a}
for some
a\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
,

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}{\cal T}_{*}^{Mj}(\rho)=p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss}.

Since
\rho\mapsto\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho)
is a norm one linear mapping, we obtain that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}|{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(nM+k,\rho)e^{i\alpha\widetilde{F}_{nM+k}(\widetilde{Q})})-{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(nM+k,p\rho^{\rm ss}_{a})e^{i\alpha\widetilde{F}_{nM+k}(\widetilde{Q})})|=0.

Notice that the collection
(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(nM,{p\rho^{\rm ss}_{a}}))_{n\geq 0}
determines a translational invariant
{\rm C}^{*}
-finitely correlated state on the coarser output chain satisfying the assumptions of Proposition 4.2 in
[
117
]
, therefore one has that if
\rho
is supported in the rank of
P_{a}
and if
{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(M+k,{p\rho^{\rm ss}_{a}})\widetilde{Q})=0
, then the convergence in law holds

\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{F}_{nM+k}(\widetilde{Q}),\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(nM+k,p\rho^{\rm ss}_{a}))\longrightarrow{\cal N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{Q,a})

with

\sigma^{2}_{Q,a}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}(nM+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})(\widetilde{F}_{nM+k}(\widetilde{Q})^{2}).

If we check that for every
a=0,\dots,p-1
,
\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(M+k,p\rho^{\rm ss}_{a})(\widetilde{Q})=0
and
\sigma_{Q,a}^{2}
does not depend on
a
and is given by the expression in Eq. (
30
), we are done. Notice that

\begin{split}{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(M+k,p\rho^{\rm ss}_{a})\widetilde{Q})&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{M}}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(M+k,p\rho^{\rm ss}_{a})\left(\sum_{l=0}^{M-1}\tau_{l}(Q)\right)\right)\\
&=\frac{p}{\sqrt{M}}\sum_{l=0}^{M-1}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(k,{\cal T}_{*}^{l}(\rho_{a}^{\rm ss}))Q\right)\\
&=\frac{pm}{\sqrt{M}}\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(k,\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})Q\right)\\
&=\sqrt{M}{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(k)Q)=0.\end{split}

We used the fact that
{\cal T}_{*}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{a})=\rho^{\rm ss}_{a\oplus 1}
, the linearity of
\rho\mapsto\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n,\rho)
and the fact that
\rho^{\rm ss}=\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}\rho^{\rm ss}_{a}
.
Moreover,

\begin{split}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(nM+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{F}_{nM+k}(\widetilde{Q})^{2}\right)&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{l,j=0}^{n-1}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(nM+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{\tau}_{l}(\widetilde{Q})\widetilde{\tau}_{j}(\widetilde{Q})\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{l=0}^{n-1}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(nM+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{\tau}_{l}(\widetilde{Q}^{2})\right)\\
&+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{0\leq l<j\leq n-1}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(nM+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{\tau}_{l}(\widetilde{Q})\circ\widetilde{\tau}_{j}(\widetilde{Q})\right)\\
&={\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(M+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{Q}^{2}\right)\\
&+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{l=0}^{n-2}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1-l}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}((j+1)M+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{Q}\circ\widetilde{\tau}_{j}(\widetilde{Q})\right)\\
&\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow+\infty]{}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(M+kp\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{Q}^{2}\right)\\
&+2\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}((j+1)M+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{Q}\circ\widetilde{\tau}_{j}(\widetilde{Q})\right),\end{split}

where
A\circ B=(AB+BA)/2.

The limit is finite since
{\rm Tr}\left(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}((j+1)M+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{Q}\circ\widetilde{\tau}_{j}(\widetilde{Q})\right)
decays exponentially with respect to
j
since
{\cal T}^{p}_{*}
restricted to
L^{1}({\cal H}_{a})
is primitive. Notice that the previous limit can be rewritten as

\begin{split}&{\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(M+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{Q}^{2}\right)+2\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}((j+1)M+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\widetilde{Q}\circ\widetilde{\tau}_{j}(\widetilde{Q})\right)\\
&=\frac{p}{M}\sum_{l,s=0}^{M-1}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(M+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\tau_{l}(Q)\tau_{s}(Q)\right)\\
&+\frac{2p}{M}\sum_{l,s=0}^{M-1}\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}((j+1)M+k,p\rho_{a}^{\rm ss})\tau_{l}(Q)\circ\tau_{jM+s}(Q)\right)\\
&={\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(k)Q^{2})+\frac{2}{m}\sum_{l=0}^{M-1}\sum_{s=0}^{M-l-1}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(s+k,\rho^{\rm ss}_{a\oplus l})Q\circ\tau_{s}(Q)\right)\\
&+\frac{2}{m}\sum_{l=0}^{M-1}\sum_{s=M-l}^{+\infty}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(s+k,\rho^{\rm ss}_{a\oplus l})Q\circ\tau_{s}(Q)\right)\\
&={\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(k)Q^{2})+\frac{2}{m}\sum_{s=0}^{+\infty}\sum_{l=0}^{M-1}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(s+k,\rho^{\rm ss}_{a\oplus l})Q\circ\tau_{s}(Q)\right)\\
&={\rm Tr}(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(k)Q)+2\sum_{s=0}^{+\infty}{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V}^{\rm out}(k+s)Q\circ\tau_{s}(Q)).\end{split}

∎

D
Proof of the characterisation of the output equivalence

In the following, we fix two isometries
V_{l}:\mathcal{H}_{l}\to\mathcal{H}_{l}\otimes\mathcal{K}
with the same space
\mathcal{K}
and possibly different spaces
\mathcal{H}_{l}
, for
l=1,2
. We let
{\cal T}_{l}:L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{l})\to L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{l})
be the corresponding channels and denote the corresponding Kraus operators by
K_{l,i}
, cf. equation (
2.3.1
). We assume that
{\cal T}_{l}
is irreducible with stationary state
\rho^{\rm ss}_{l}
.
We now introduce a contractive (non-positive) map

{\cal T}_{12}:L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{1})\to L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}),\quad{\cal T}_{12}(X)=V_{1}^{*}(X\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V_{2}=\sum_{i}K_{1,i}^{*}XK_{2,i}.

(79)

The following Proposition characterizes the eigenvectors of
{\cal T}_{12}
corresponding to peripheral eigenvalues. A similar result was proved for
primitive
channels in
[
86
, Lemma 1]
; here we show that with a slight modification, the proof works also in the irreducible case.

Proposition 8
.

The following statements are equivalent:

1.

{\cal T}_{12}
admits an eigenvalue
c\in\mathbb{C}
of modulus one;

2.

there exist a unitary operator
W:{\cal H}_{2}\rightarrow{\cal H}_{1}
and a complex number
c
with modulus
1
satisfying the intertwining relations

W\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}=\rho^{\rm ss}_{1}W,\quad WK_{2,i}=cK_{1,i}W,\quad\text{ for all }i=1,\ldots,k.

(80)

In this case,
{\cal T}_{12}(W)=cW
;

3.

there exist a unitary operator
W:{\cal H}_{2}\rightarrow{\cal H}_{1}
and a complex number
c
with modulus
1
satisfying the intertwining relations

(W\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V_{2}=cV_{1}W.

(81)

In this case,
{\cal T}_{12}(W)=cW
.

The implication
2.\Rightarrow 1.
and the equivalence
2.\Leftrightarrow 3.
are trivial. We are going to show that
1.
implies
2.
as well.
Let us consider
F\in L^{\infty}({\cal H}_{2},{\cal H}_{1})
such that
{\cal T}_{12}(F)=cF
; without any loss of generality we can assume that
\|F^{*}F\|_{\infty}=1
. Since
V_{1}
is an isometry, we have

V_{1}V_{1}^{*}\leq\mathbf{1}
. Hence,

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{2}(F^{*}F)

\displaystyle=V_{2}^{*}(F^{*}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})(F\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V_{2}\geq V_{2}^{*}(F^{*}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V_{1}V_{1}^{*}(F\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V_{2}

\displaystyle={\cal T}_{12}(F)^{*}{\cal T}_{12}(F)=|c|^{2}F^{*}F=F^{*}F,

so that
{\cal T}_{2}^{n}(F^{*}F)\geq F^{*}F
for all

n\in\mathbb{N}
by the positivity of
{\cal T}_{2}
. Therefore,
{\cal S}_{n}(F^{*}F)\geq F^{*}F
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
{\cal S}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}{\cal T}_{2}^{k}
.
Let
P
be the projection onto the eigenspace of
F^{*}F
corresponding to its largest eigenvalue
\|F^{*}F\|_{\infty}=1
. Using (
19
) (as
{\cal T}_{2*}
is irreducible), we have

{\rm tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}F^{*}F)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\rm tr}(P)^{-1}{\rm tr}(P{\cal S}_{n}(F^{*}F))\geq{\rm tr}(P)^{-1}{\rm tr}(PF^{*}F)=\|F^{*}F\|_{\infty}=1.

Now, since
{\rm tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}F^{*}F)\leq{\rm tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{2})\|F^{*}F\|_{\infty}=\|F^{*}F\|_{\infty}=1
, in any case, we obtain

{\rm tr}[\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}F^{*}F]=1
, i.e.
P=\mathbf{1}
and
F^{*}F=\mathbf{1}_{{\cal H}_{1}}
.
Now fix a unit vector
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
, then one has

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\langle K_{1,i}F\varphi|FK_{2,i}\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi|F^{*}T_{12}(F)\varphi\rangle=c\langle\varphi|F^{*}F\varphi\rangle=c,

(82)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\|K_{1,i}F\varphi\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\langle F\varphi|K_{1,i}^{*}K_{1,i}F\varphi\rangle=\langle\varphi|F^{*}F\varphi\rangle=1,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\|FK_{2,i}\varphi\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\langle K_{2,i}\varphi|F^{*}FK_{2,i}\varphi\rangle=\sum_{i}\langle K_{2,i}\varphi|K_{2,i}\varphi\rangle=1.

Hence,

1=\left|\sum_{i}\langle K_{1,i}F\varphi|FK_{2,i}\varphi\rangle\right|^{2}\leq\sum_{i}\|K_{1,i}F\varphi\|^{2}\sum_{i}\|FK_{2,i}\varphi\|^{2}=1.

This shows that Cauchy-Schwartz inequality is saturated, and hence there is a constant
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
such that

FK_{2,i}\varphi=\lambda K_{1,i}F\varphi\text{ for all }\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{2},\,i=1,\ldots,k.

(83)

Putting this back in (
82
), we see that
\lambda=c
. Next we show that
\rho^{\rm ss}_{1}=F\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}F^{*}
. To that end we define
\rho^{\rm ss}:=F\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}F^{*}
; then
\rho^{\rm ss}
is positive, and
{\rm tr}(\rho^{\rm ss})={\rm tr}(F^{*}F\rho^{\rm ss}_{2})=1
, so
\rho^{\rm ss}
is a state. We have
K_{1,i}F\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}(K_{1,i}F)^{*}=FK_{2,i}\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}(FK_{2,i})^{*}
for each
i
by (
83
), and hence

{\cal T}_{1*}(\rho^{\rm ss})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{1,i}F\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}(K_{1,i}F)^{*}=F{\cal T}_{2*}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{2})F^{*}=F\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}F^{*}=\rho^{\rm ss},

so by irreducibility,
\rho^{\rm ss}
is the unique stationary state of
{\cal T}_{1*}
, that is,

F\rho^{\rm ss}_{2}F^{*}=\rho^{\rm ss}_{1}.

(84)

∎

The following lemma provides information on the structure of the output state.

Lemma 10
.

Consider an irreducible QMC defined by an isometry
V
, and let
\rho^{\rm ss}
be the stationary state with spectral decomposition

\rho^{\rm ss}=\sum_{m=1}^{d}\pi_{m}|\phi_{m}\rangle\langle\phi_{m}|
.
Then

(a)

The stationary output state has the form

\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n):=\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}\pi_{p}|\psi_{mp}(n)\rangle\langle\psi_{mp}(n)|,

where
\psi_{mp}(n)=\sum_{{\bf i}\in I^{(n)}}\langle\phi_{m}|{\bf K}^{(n)}_{\bf i}\phi_{p}\rangle|{\bf i}\rangle
for each
m,p=1,\ldots,d
.

(b)

The rank of
\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n)
does not exceed
d^{2}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
{\rm tr}((\rho^{\rm out}_{V}(n))^{2})\geq d^{-2}
.

We have

V^{(n)}\rho^{\rm ss}V^{(n)*}=\sum_{p=1}^{d}\pi_{p}\sum_{{\bf i},{\bf i}^{\prime}\in I^{(n)}}|K^{(n)}_{\bf i}\phi_{p}\rangle\langle K^{(n)}_{{\bf i}^{\prime}}\phi_{p}|\otimes|{\bf i}\rangle\langle{\bf i}^{\prime}|.

Moreover, we have

{\rm tr}(|K^{(n)}_{\bf i}\phi_{p}\rangle\langle K^{(n)}_{{\bf i}^{\prime}}\phi_{p}|)=\sum_{m=1}^{d}\langle\phi_{m}|K^{(n)}_{\bf i}\phi_{p}\rangle\overline{\langle\phi_{m}|K^{(n)}_{{\bf i}^{\prime}}\phi_{p}\rangle}.

Part (a) follows from this. To prove (b) let
Q_{n}
and
r_{n}
be the support projection and the rank of
\rho_{U}^{out}(n)
, respectively. From (a) we see that
r_{n}\leq d^{2}
(where the latter is just the number of vectors
\psi_{m^{\prime}m}(n)
), so
{\rm tr}((\rho_{V}^{out}(n))^{2})\geq{\rm tr}(\rho_{U}^{out}(n)(Q_{n}/\sqrt{r_{n}}))^{2}=1/r_{n}\geq 1/d^{2}
.
∎

The following lemma connects the notion of output equivalence to the spectrum of the map
{\cal T}_{12}
defined in Eq. (
79
).

Lemma 11
.

(a)

Let
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be two isometries, and let
\rho^{\rm ss}_{l}=\sum_{i}\pi_{l,i}|\phi_{l,i}\rangle\langle\phi_{l,i}|
be the spectral decompositions of their stationary states, for
l=1,2
.
The overlap of the corresponding output states is given by

{\rm tr}(\rho_{V_{1}}^{\rm out}(n)\rho_{V_{2}}^{\rm out}(n))=\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}\sum_{m^{\prime},p^{\prime}=1}^{d}\pi_{1,p}\pi_{2,p^{\prime}}|\langle\phi_{1,p}|{\cal T}_{12}^{n}(|\phi_{1,m}\rangle\langle\phi_{2,m^{\prime}}|)|\phi_{2,p^{\prime}}\rangle|^{2}.

(b)

If
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are equivalent, then the map
{\cal T}_{12}
has an eigenvalue of modulus one.

Define
\psi_{l,mp}(n)=\sum_{{\bf i}\in I^{(n)}}\langle\phi_{l,m}|{\bf K}^{(n)}_{l,{\bf i}}\phi_{l,p}\rangle|{\bf i}\rangle
for each
m,p=1,\ldots,d
, and
l=1,2
. Using part (a) of the previous Lemma, we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm tr}(\rho_{V_{1}}^{\rm out}(n)\rho_{V_{2}}^{\rm out}(n))

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,p=1}^{d}\sum_{m^{\prime},p^{\prime}=1}^{d}\pi_{1,p}\pi_{2,p^{\prime}}|\langle\psi_{1,mp}(n)|\psi_{2,m^{\prime}p^{\prime}}(n)\rangle|^{2},

which has the form claimed in (a), because

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1,mp}(n)|\psi_{2,m^{\prime}p^{\prime}}(n)\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bf i}\in I^{(n)}}\overline{\langle\phi_{1,m}|{\bf K}_{1,\bf i}^{(n)}\phi_{1,p}\rangle}\langle\phi_{2,m^{\prime}}|{\bf K}_{2,\bf i}^{(n)}\phi_{2,p^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{1,p}|{\cal T}_{12}^{n}(|\phi_{1,m}\rangle\langle\phi_{2m^{\prime}}|)|\phi_{2,p^{\prime}}\rangle.

In order to prove (b), recall from the previous section that the spectral radius of
{\cal T}_{12}
does not exceed 1. Hence, if
{\cal T}_{12}
does not have any eigenvalue of modulus one, then all its eigenvalues have modulus strictly less than 1, which (by the standard Jordan decomposition) implies
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\cal T}_{12}^{n}=0
, and hence
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\rm tr}(\rho_{V_{1}}^{\rm out}(n)\rho_{V_{2}}^{\rm out}(n))=0
by (a). But then the output states cannot be equal, because
{\rm tr}((\rho_{V_{l}}^{\rm out}(n))^{2})
must be bounded away from zero by part (b) of Lemma
10
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the characterisation of the output equivalence.

Since (iii) is just a reformulation of (ii), it suffices to show that (i) is equivalent to (ii). From the explicit form of the stationary output state, one can easily verify that (ii) implies (i). In order to prove the other implication, first note that (i) implies that the map
T_{12}
defined in equation (
79
) has eigenvalue of modulus one by part (b) of Lemma
11
, which implies (ii) by Proposition
8
.
∎

E
Proofs of the results in Section
4

The nontrivial part consists of showing that
\mathcal{P}^{\rm irr}=\mathscr{V}^{\rm irr}/\mathscr{G}
and proving that the action is almost free. After proving this, one can transfer the orbifold structure of
\mathscr{V}^{\rm irr}/\mathscr{G}
(Proposition 1.5.1 in
[
32
]
) to
\mathscr{P}^{\rm irr}
.

Theorem
6
shows that
V
and
V^{\prime}
are output equivalent if and only if there exists
c\in U(1)
, and
W\in PU(d)
such that

V^{\prime}=\overline{c}(W\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})VW^{*},

(85)

which holds exactly if
V^{\prime}=g\cdot V
for some
g=(c,W)
. This shows that the orbit of any given
V
under the action of
\mathscr{G}
coincides exactly with the equivalence class
[V]\in\mathcal{P}
. This means that
\mathcal{P}=\mathscr{V}^{\rm irr}/\mathscr{G}
.

In order to show the rest of the claim we need to prove that the group action
\mu
defined above is smooth, proper and almost free (see e.g. Proposition 1.5.1
[
32
]
). To that end, we first note that
g\mapsto g\cdot V
is clearly smooth with respect to
W
as an element of
U(d)
, and hence also the lift to
PU(d)
is smooth. Since
\mathscr{G}
is compact, the action is proper. Let us prove that the action is almost free. Suppose that
g\cdot V=g^{\prime}V
for some
V
and
g=(c,W)
,
g^{\prime}=(c^{\prime},W^{\prime})
. This implies, first of all, that
c\overline{c}^{\prime}VW^{\prime*}W=(W^{\prime*}W\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V
, and consequently

{\cal T}_{V}(W^{\prime*}W)=V^{*}(W^{\prime*}W\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V=c\overline{c}^{\prime}V^{*}VW^{\prime*}W=c\overline{c}^{\prime}W^{\prime*}W,

that is,
W^{\prime*}W
is an eigenvector of
{\cal T}_{V}
with eigenvalue
c\overline{c}^{\prime}
. Since this has modulus one, irreducibility implies that
c\overline{c}^{\prime}=\gamma_{V}^{k}
and
W^{\prime*}W=Z_{V}^{k}
for some
k=0,\dots,p_{V}-1
. Hence
c=c^{\prime}\gamma^{k}
and
W=W^{\prime}Z^{k}
as elements of
PU(d)
.
Hence
g=g^{\prime}(\gamma_{V}^{k},Z_{V}^{k})
and

\mathscr{G}_{V}=\{(\gamma_{V}^{k},Z_{V}^{k}):\,k=0,\dots,p_{V}-1\}.

∎

If we consider a Riemannian metric
\nu
on
\mathscr{V}^{\rm irr}
such that for every
V\in\mathscr{V}^{\rm irr}

T^{\rm id}_{V}\perp T^{\rm nonid}_{V},

then we can consider the new metric

\nu^{\prime}_{V}(X,Y):=\int_{\mathscr{G}}\nu_{g\cdot V}(d_{V}\mu^{g}(X),d_{V}\mu^{g}(Y))d\xi(g),\quad X,Y\in T_{V}(\mathscr{V}^{\rm irr}),

where
\xi
is the unique normalized right invariant Haar measure on
\mathscr{G}
; it is trivial to verify that
\mu^{g}
is an isometry for
\nu^{\prime}
for every
g\in\mathscr{G}
and Lemma
5
ensures that
T^{\rm id}_{V}\perp T^{\rm nonid}_{V}
under
\nu^{\prime}
as well.

In order to conclude, we need to show that there exists a metric
\nu
with the property above. A possibility is given by

\nu_{V}(X,Y)=\langle\omega_{V}(X),\omega_{V}(Y)\rangle+2\real({\rm Tr}(\rho_{V}^{\rm ss}P_{V}(X)^{*}P_{V}(Y))),

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is any inner product on
\{(\theta,K):\,\theta\in\mathbb{R},\,K\in L^{\infty}({\cal H}),\,K^{*}=K\}
.

Finally, we only need to check that averaging over
\mathscr{G}
does not change the value of
\nu_{V}(A,A)
for
A\in T^{\rm id}_{V}
. Notice that for every
A\in T^{\rm id}_{V}
,
g\in\mathscr{G}
one has

\begin{split}\nu_{g\cdot V}(d_{V}\mu^{g}(A),d_{V}\mu^{g}(A))&=\nu_{g\cdot V}(d_{V}\mu^{g}(P_{V}(A)),d_{V}\mu^{g}(P_{V}(A)))\\
&=\nu_{g\cdot V}(P_{g\cdot V}d_{V}\mu^{g}(A),P_{g\cdot V}d_{V}\mu^{g}(A))\\
&={\rm Tr}(\rho_{g\cdot V}^{\rm ss}d_{V}\mu^{g}(A)^{*}d_{V}\mu^{g}(A))={\rm Tr}(\rho_{V}^{\rm ss}A^{*}A)=\nu_{V}(A,A).\end{split}

We used that for
g=(c,W)
,
\rho^{\rm ss}_{g\cdot V}=W\rho^{\rm ss}_{V}W^{*}
and Lemma
5
.
∎

In order to prove the first two statements, we only need to determine the vector subspace of
T^{\rm id}_{V}
that is fixed by
d_{V}\mu^{g}
for every
g\in\mathscr{G}_{V}
. Let us consider
A\in T^{\rm id}_{V}
, then
d_{V}\mu^{g}(A)=A
for
g=(\gamma_{V}^{-k},Z_{V}^{*k})
if and only if

\gamma_{V}^{k}(Z_{V}^{*k}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})AZ_{V}^{k}=A.

(86)

Let us consider the spectral resolution of
Z_{V}
:

Z_{V}^{k}=\sum_{a=0}^{p_{[V]}-1}\gamma^{ak}P_{a}(V)

and notice that condition in Eq. (
86
) implies that

A=\sum_{a=0}^{p_{[V]}-1}(P_{a\oplus 1}(V)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})AP_{a}(V).

(87)

Indeed, plugging in the spectral resolution of
Z_{V}^{k}
in Eq. (
86
) one obtains that the following equation

\sum_{a,b=0}^{p_{V}-1}\gamma_{V}^{k(a-b+1)}(P_{b}(V)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})AP_{a}(V)=\sum_{a,b=0}^{p_{V}-1}(P_{b}(V)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})AP_{a}(V)

needs to hold for every
k=0,\dots,p_{V}-1
. Using that
\gamma_{V}=e^{i2\pi/p_{V}}
and that

\{P_{a}(V):a=0,\dots,p-1\}
is a resolution of the identity, one gets that
P_{b}(V)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}AP_{a}(V)
can only be different from zero when
a-b+1\equiv_{p}0
, i.e.
b\equiv_{p}a+1
.

Moreover, note that equation (
49
) is equivalent to

VP_{a}(V)V^{*}=(P_{a\oplus 1}(V)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})VV^{*},

which shows that
(P_{a\oplus 1}(V)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})
and
VV^{*}
are commuting projections and that the dimension of the range of
(P_{a\oplus 1}(V)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})VV^{*}
is equal to
d_{a}([V])
, therefore the dimension of the range of
(P_{a\oplus 1}(V)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})(\mathbf{1}_{{\cal H}\otimes{\cal K}}-VV^{*})
is equal to
d_{a\oplus 1}([V])k-d_{a}([V])
. Since the range of
A\in T^{\rm id}_{V}
is included in
{\rm Range}(V)^{\perp}
(cf. Proposition
4
), the real dimension of those tangent vectors which satisfy condition in Eq. (
87
) is equal to

2\left(\sum_{a=0}^{p_{[V]}-1}(d_{a\oplus 1}([V])k-d_{a}([V]))d_{a}([V])\right).

Let us prove the last statement:
p_{[V]}=p_{[W]}
follows from the fact that elements laying in the same submanifold of the canonical stratification have the same local group. Moreover, if
[V]
and
[W]
are two points sitting on the same connected component of the submanifold
\mathscr{P}_{p,l}
, then they must be connected by a smooth path
\alpha:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{p,l}
. Since an atlas for
\mathscr{P}_{p,l}
can be obtained restricting the collection of fundamental charts we built in Subsection
4.3
, one can easily see that the path
\alpha
can be lifted to a path
\widetilde{\alpha}
in
\mathscr{V}^{\rm irr}
. Let us consider the smooth family of quantum channels
(T_{\widetilde{\alpha}(t)})_{t\in[0,1]}
: since peripheral eigenvalues are algebraically simple, perturbation theory ensures that the family of matrices
Z_{\widetilde{\alpha}(t)}
is smooth as well. Using the fact that

P_{a}(\widetilde{\alpha}(t))=\sum_{m=0}^{p(\widetilde{\alpha}(t))-1}\overline{\gamma}_{\widetilde{\alpha}(t)}^{am}Z_{\widetilde{\alpha}(t)}^{m},

we can deduce that the family of projections
P_{a}(\widetilde{\alpha}(t))
is smooth as well, therefore
d_{a}(\widetilde{\alpha}(t))
must be constant for all
t\in[0,1]
.
∎

F
Proofs of local approximations

Thanks to the fact that QFI is a nonnegative bilinear form, in order to show that the limit of the scaled QFI only depends on the identifiable component, it is enough to show that for every
A\in T^{\rm nonid}_{V_{0}}
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{F_{V_{0},n}(A,A)}{n}=0.

(88)

Let us write the explicit expression of
F_{V_{0},n}(A,A)
for any
A\in T_{V_{0}}(\mathscr{V}^{\rm irr})
: we recall that

\ket{\partial_A\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_0}(n)}:=i\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{0}^{(n)}\cdots V_{0}^{(i+1)}AV_{0}^{(i-1)}\cdots V_{0}^{(1)}\ket{\varphi}

and that, for pure statistical models, one has

F_{V_{0},n}(A,A)=4\left(\|\partial_{A}\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_{0}}(n)\|^{2}-\left|\bra{\partial_A\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_0}(n)}\ket{\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_0}(n)}\right|^{2}\right).

It is easy to verify that

\langle\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_{0}}(n)|\partial_{A}\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_{0}}(n)\rangle=i\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\langle\varphi|{\cal T}_{V_{0}}^{i}(V_{0}^{*}A)\varphi\rangle

and

\begin{split}\|\partial_{A}\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_{0}}(n)\|^{2}&=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\langle\varphi|{\cal T}_{V_{0}}^{i}(A^{*}A)\varphi\rangle\quad(I)\\
&+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\langle\varphi|{\cal T}_{V_{0}}^{i-1}(A^{*}{\cal T}_{V_{0}}^{j-i-1}(V_{0}^{*}A)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{{\cal K}}V_{0})\varphi\rangle\quad(II)\\
&+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\langle\varphi|{\cal T}^{i-1}(V_{0}^{*}{\cal T}_{V_{0}}^{j-i-1}(A^{*}V_{0})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{{\cal K}}A)\varphi\rangle.\quad(III)\end{split}

Note that non identifiable tangent vectors are of the form
\theta V_{0}-(K\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V_{0}+V_{0}K
for some
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and for
K\in L^{\infty}({\cal H})
such that
K=K^{*}
and
{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}K)=0
.

Case 1.
Let us first consider a vector of the form
A=\theta V_{0}
; in this case, using that
V_{0}^{*}V_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{\cal H}
and that
{\cal T}_{V_{0}}
is unital, one immediately sees that
F_{V_{0},n}(A,A)=0
and we are done.

Case 2.
Let us now consider
A=-(K\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V_{0}+V_{0}K.

In this case
V_{0}^{*}A=({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K)
and

A^{*}A={\cal T}_{V_{0}}(K^{2})-{\cal T}_{V_{0}}(K)K-K{\cal T}_{V_{0}}(K)+K^{2}.

Therefore,

\langle\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_{0}}(n)|\partial_{A}\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_{0}}(n)\rangle=i\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\langle\varphi|{\cal T}_{V_{0}}^{i}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K)\varphi\rangle=i\langle\varphi|({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}}^{n})(K)\varphi\rangle

and

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{|\langle\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_{0}}(n)|\partial_{A}\Psi^{\rm s+o}_{V_{0}}(n)\rangle|^{2}}{n}=0.

On the other hand,

\begin{split}(I)&=n{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}({\cal T}_{V_{0}}(K^{2})-{\cal T}_{V_{0}}(K)K-K{\cal T}_{V_{0}}(K)+K^{2}))+O(1)\\
&=n{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K)K+K({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K)))+O(1).\end{split}

Using the spectral decomposition of
{\cal T}_{V_{0}}
as

{\cal T}_{V_{0}}=\sum_{a=0}^{p_{V_{0}}}\gamma_{V_{0}}^{a}\ket{Z_{V_0}^{a}}_{HS}\bra{J^*_{V_0,a}}_{HS}+{\cal R}

with
Z^{a}_{V_{0}},J_{V_{0},a}
given by (
22
) and
\|{\cal R}^{n}\|\leq C\zeta^{n}
where
C>0
and
\zeta\in(0,1)
, one can write

\begin{split}(II)&=\sum_{a,b=0}^{p_{V_{0}}-1}\langle\varphi|Z_{V_{0}}^{a}\varphi\rangle\langle J^{*}_{V_{0},a}|A^{*}Z_{V_{0}}^{b}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}\rangle_{HS}\langle J^{*}_{V_{0},b}|V_{0}^{*}A\rangle_{HS}\cdot\left(\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\gamma_{V_{0}}^{a(i-1)+b(j-i-1)}\right)\\
&+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(A^{*}{\cal R}^{j-i-1}(V_{0}^{*}A)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0})\\
&+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\langle\varphi|{\cal R}^{i-1}(A^{*}V_{0})\varphi{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}V_{0}^{*}A)\varphi\rangle+O(1).\end{split}

where in the middle term we separated the two contributions from
\mathcal{T}
and used the contractivity property of
\mathcal{R}
.
Note that
{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}V_{0}^{*}A)=0
and the last term in the previous equation disappears. Let us rewrite the second term as well as

\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n-i-1}{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(A^{*}{\cal R}^{k}(V_{0}^{*}A)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0})=n{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(A^{*}({\rm Id}-{\cal R})^{-1}(V_{0}^{*}A)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0})+O(1).\end{split}

Let us now focus on the first term: notice that if
a=b
, then using that
Z_{V_{0}}^{a}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}=\gamma_{V_{0}}^{a}V_{0}Z_{V_{0}}^{a}
and that
J_{V_{0},a}Z_{V_{0}}^{a}=\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}
, one has

\langle J^{*}_{V_{0},a}|A^{*}Z_{V_{0}}^{a}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}\rangle_{HS}=\gamma_{V_{0}}^{a}\langle J^{*}_{V_{0},a}|A^{*}V_{0}Z_{V_{0}}^{a}\rangle_{HS}=\gamma_{V_{0}}^{a}{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}A^{*}V_{0})=0.

Likewise, if
b=0
, then

\langle J^{*}_{V_{0},b}|V_{0}^{*}A\rangle_{HS}={\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}V_{0}^{*}A)=0.

On the other hand, if
a\neq b
and
b\neq 0
, then

\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\gamma_{V_{0}}^{a(i-1)+b(j-i-1)}=\begin{cases}(1-\gamma_{V_{0}}^{b})^{-1}n+O(1)\text{ if}a=0,\,b\neq 0\\
O(1)\text{ in all the other cases.}\end{cases}

Therefore the first term in
(II)
is

n\sum_{b=1}^{p_{V_{0}}-1}(1-\gamma_{V_{0}}^{b})^{-1}\langle{J^{*}_{V_{0},b}}|V_{0}^{*}A\rangle_{HS}{\rm Tr}\left(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}A^{*}Z_{V_{0}}^{b}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}\right)+O(1).

Putting everything together and using
V_{0}^{*}A=({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K)
, we obtain that

\begin{split}(II)&=n{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}A^{*}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})^{-1}(V_{0}^{*}A)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0})+O(1)\\
&=n{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}A^{*}(K\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V_{0})+O(1)\\
&={\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(K({\cal T}_{V_{0}}-{\rm Id})(K))+O(1).\end{split}

Observing that
(III)
is just the complex conjugate of
(II)
we are done.

Let us focus on the limit of the scaled QFI along identifiable directions: let us consider
A\in T^{\rm id}_{V_{0}}
; remembering that (see Proposition
4
) one has
V_{0}^{*}A=0
, then

\frac{1}{n}F_{V_{0},n}(A,A)=\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\langle\varphi|{\cal T}_{V_{0}}^{i}(A^{*}A)\varphi\rangle\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow+\infty]{}4{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}A^{*}A)=F_{V_{0}}(A,A)

and the statement follows from polarisation identities.
∎

Both statistical models are pure, therefore we will show that for
X,Y\in B_{r_{n}}(V_{0})\subset T_{V_{0}}(\mathscr{V}^{\rm irr})
one has

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}|\langle\Psi_{V(Xn^{-1/2})}(n)|\Psi_{V(Yn^{-1/2})}(n)\rangle-\langle W(X^{\rm id})\Omega|W(Y^{\rm id})\Omega\rangle|=0

uniformly in
X
and
Y
and with a suitable rate in order to apply Lemma
2
. We recall that

\langle W(X^{\rm id})\Omega|W(Y^{\rm id})\Omega\rangle=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{V_{0}}(X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id},X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id})+i\sigma_{V_{0}}(X^{\rm id},Y^{\rm id})},

therefore

\begin{split}1-|\langle W(X^{\rm id})\Omega|W(Y^{\rm id})\Omega\rangle|^{2}&=1-e^{-\beta_{V_{0}}(X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id},X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id})}\leq F_{V_{0}}(X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id},X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id})\\
&\leq\nu_{V_{0}}(X-Y,X-Y)\end{split}

and condition
1
in Lemma
2
holds, with
\alpha=2,C=1
.
Using the explicit expression of the system and output state (Eq. (
26
)) we obtain

\begin{split}&\langle\Psi_{V(Xn^{-1/2})}(n)|\Psi_{V(Yn^{-1/2})}(n)\rangle=\langle\varphi|{\cal T}_{X,Y,n}^{n}(\mathbf{1}_{\cal H})\varphi\rangle,\\
&{\cal T}_{X,Y,n}(A):=V(Xn^{-1/2})^{*}(A\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V(Yn^{-1/2}).\end{split}

Note that

\begin{split}&V(Xn^{-1/2})=V_{0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}iX+\frac{1}{2n}\partial^{2}_{X}V(0)+O(n^{3(\delta-1/2)}),\\
&V(Yn^{-1/2})=V_{0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}iY+\frac{1}{2n}\partial^{2}_{Y}V(0)+O(n^{3(\delta-1/2)}).\end{split}

where the Taylor expansions are taken seeing
V
as a smooth mappings from
B_{\epsilon}(V)
to
L^{\infty}({\cal H},{\cal H}\otimes{\cal K})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2d^{2}k}
; the first order approximation is given by
iX,iY
because the differential of
V
in
0
is the identity mapping (the factor
i
in front comes from the fact that we removed it in the definition of the abstract tangent space in equation (
35
). We remark that the reminder is
O(n^{3(\delta-1/2)})
uniformly in
X
and
Y
in
B_{Cn^{\delta}}(0)
. Consequently, one has

{\cal T}_{X,Y,n}={\cal T}_{V_{0}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}+\frac{1}{2n}{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}+O(n^{3(\delta-1/2)}),

where

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(A)

\displaystyle=-iX^{*}A\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}+iV_{0}^{*}A\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y,

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}(A)

\displaystyle=\partial^{2}_{X}V(0)A\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}+2X^{*}A\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y+V_{0}^{*}A\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}\partial^{2}_{Y}V(0).

We now follow similar steps as in Theorem 2 in
[
77
]
, but reproduce the calculations in detail in order to carefully track the order of the remainder. Thanks to the irreducibility of
{\cal T}_{V_{0}}
, perturbation theory ensures that for
n
big enough
{\cal T}_{X,Y,n}
admits an algebraically simple eigenvalue
\lambda_{X,Y,n}
with the largest absolute value; moreover, if we call
x_{X,Y,n}
the corresponding eigenvector (normalized in a way that
{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}x_{X,Y,n})\equiv 1
), one has that

\begin{split}\lambda_{X,Y,n}=1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\dot{\lambda}_{X,Y}+\frac{1}{2n}\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}+O(n^{3(\delta-1/2)}),\\
x_{X,Y,n}=\mathbf{1}_{\cal H}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\dot{x}_{X,Y}+\frac{1}{2n}\ddot{x}_{X,Y}+O(n^{3(\delta-1/2)}).\end{split}

Differentiating in
0
the eigenvalue equation
{\cal T}_{X,Y,n}(x_{X,Y,n})=\lambda_{X,Y,n}x_{X,Y,n}
one obtains

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(\mathbf{1}_{\cal H})+{\cal T}_{V_{0}}(\dot{x}_{X,Y})=\dot{x}_{X,Y}+\dot{\lambda}_{X,Y}\mathbf{1}_{\cal H},

(89)

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}(\mathbf{1}_{\cal H})+2{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(\dot{x}_{X,Y})+{\cal T}_{V_{0}}(\ddot{x}_{X,Y})=\ddot{x}_{X,Y}+2\dot{\lambda}_{X,Y}\dot{x}_{X,Y}+\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}\mathbf{1}_{\cal H}.

(90)

Note that

{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(\mathbf{1}_{\cal H})=-iX^{*}V_{0}+iV_{0}^{*}Y=-iX^{{\rm noid}*}V_{0}+iV_{0}^{*}Y^{\rm noid}

because of Proposition
4
. Moreover, we recall that
X^{\rm noid}
is of the form
\theta_{X}V_{0}-(K_{X}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K})V_{0}+V_{0}K
and the same holds for
Y
; without loss of generality, we can we can assume that
\theta_{X}=\theta_{Y}=0
: indeed, if this is not the case, one can consider
e^{-i\theta_{X}n^{-1/2}}V(Xn^{-1/2})
and
e^{-i\theta_{Y}n^{-1/2}}V(Yn^{-1/2})
instead of
V(Xn^{-1/2})
and
V(Yn^{-1/2})
(notice that the states considered do not change). Therefore,

{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(\mathbf{1}_{\cal H})=i({\rm Id}-{\cal T})(K_{Y}-K_{X}).

On the other hand, by differentiating

{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}x_{X,Y,n})\equiv 1
, we obtain
{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}\dot{x}_{X,Y})=0
and plugging into (
89
) we get

\dot{\lambda}_{X,Y}=0
and

\dot{x}_{X,Y}=i(K_{Y}-K_{X}).

Equation (
90
) becomes

\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}\mathbf{1}_{\cal H}={\cal T}_{X,Y,2}(\mathbf{1}_{\cal H})+2{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(\dot{x}_{X,Y})+({\cal T}_{V_{0}}-{\rm Id})(\ddot{x}_{X,Y})

and if we evaluate the state corresponding to
\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}
at both sides we obtain that

\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}={\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}(\mathbf{1}))+2{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(\dot{x}_{X,Y})).

Notice that
{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}(\mathbf{1}))={\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(\partial^{2}_{X}V(0)^{*}V_{0}+2X^{*}Y+V_{0}^{*}\partial^{2}_{Y}V(0)));
using that
V(\cdot)^{*}V(\cdot)\equiv\mathbf{1}_{\cal H}
, one has that

\partial^{2}_{X}V(0)^{*}V_{0}+V_{0}^{*}\partial^{2}_{X}V(0)=-2X^{*}X.

Therefore,

\partial^{2}_{X}V(0)^{*}V_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial^{2}_{X}V(0)^{*}V_{0}+V_{0}^{*}\partial^{2}_{X}V(0))+i\left(\frac{1}{2i}\partial^{2}_{X}V(0)^{*}V_{0}-\frac{1}{2i}V_{0}^{*}\partial^{2}_{X}V(0)\right)

and we can write

{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}(\mathbf{1}))=-{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(X^{*}X-2X^{*}Y+Y^{*}Y))+i\alpha_{X}-i\alpha_{Y},

where
\alpha_{X},\alpha_{Y}
are irrelevant phases that can be absorbed in the states. Moreover,

\begin{split}&-{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(X^{*}X-2X^{*}Y+Y^{*}Y))\\
&=-{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(X^{{\rm id}*}X^{\rm id}-2X^{{\rm id}*}Y^{\rm id}+Y^{{\rm id}*}Y^{\rm id}))\\
&+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}X^{{\rm id}*}K_{X}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0})+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}V_{0}^{*}K_{X}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}X^{\rm id})\\
&-{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{X})K_{X}+K_{X}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{X}))\\
&+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}Y^{{\rm id}*}K_{Y}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0})+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}V_{0}^{*}K_{Y}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y^{\rm id})\\
&-{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{Y})K_{Y}+K_{Y}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{Y}))\\
&-2{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}X^{{\rm id}*}K_{Y}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0})\\
&-2{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}V_{0}^{*}K_{X}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y^{\rm id})\\
&+2{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{X})K_{Y}+K_{X}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{Y})).\end{split}

On the other hand,

\begin{split}&{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(\dot{x}_{X,Y}))\\
&={\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}X^{*}(K_{Y}-K_{X})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}+V_{0}^{*}(K_{Y}-K_{X})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y)\\
&={\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}X^{{\rm id}*}(K_{Y}-K_{X})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0})\\
&-{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}K_{X}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{Y})+K_{X}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{X}))\\
&+{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}V_{0}^{*}(K_{X}-K_{Y})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y^{\rm id})\\
&-{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{X})K_{Y})+{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{Y})K_{Y}).\end{split}

Summing up, one gets

\begin{split}&-{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(X^{*}X-2X^{*}Y+Y^{*}Y)+2{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(\dot{x}_{X,Y}))\\
&=-{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(X^{{\rm id}*}X^{\rm id}-2X^{{\rm id}*}Y^{\rm id}+Y^{{\rm id}*}Y^{\rm id}))\\
&+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(V_{0}^{*}K_{X}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}X^{\rm id}-X^{{\rm id}*}K_{X}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}))\\
&+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(K_{X}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{X})-({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{X})K_{X})\\
&+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(Y^{{\rm id}*}K_{Y}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}-V_{0}^{*}K_{Y}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y^{\rm id}))\\
&+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{Y})K_{Y}-K_{Y}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{Y})).\end{split}

Notice that

\begin{split}&{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(V_{0}^{*}K_{X}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}X^{\rm id}-X^{{\rm id}*}K_{X}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}))\\
&+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(K_{X}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{X})-({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{X})K_{X})\end{split}

and

\begin{split}&{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(Y^{{\rm id}*}K_{Y}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}-V_{0}^{*}K_{Y}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y^{\rm id}))\\
&+{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{Y})K_{Y}-K_{Y}({\rm Id}-{\cal T}_{V_{0}})(K_{Y})).\end{split}

are purely imaginary and depend on
X
and
Y
only, respectively. Therefore, they can be absorbed in
i\alpha_{X}
and
i\alpha_{Y}
and we can finally write

\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}=-{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(X^{{\rm id}*}X^{\rm id}-2X^{{\rm id}*}Y^{\rm id}+Y^{{\rm id}*}Y^{\rm id}))+i\alpha_{X}-i\alpha_{Y}.

From the definition and the properties of
\beta_{V_{0}}
and
\sigma_{V_{0}}
one has

{\rm Tr}(\rho_{V_{0}}^{\rm ss}(X^{{\rm id}*}X^{\rm id}-2X^{{\rm id}*}Y^{\rm id}+Y^{{\rm id}*}Y^{\rm id}))=\beta_{V_{0}}(X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id},X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id})+2i\sigma_{V_{0}}(X^{\rm id},Y^{\rm id}).

We are finally ready to prove the limit and establish the rate of decay of the error: for
n
big enough one has

\begin{split}|\langle\varphi|{\cal T}_{X,Y,n}^{n}(\mathbf{1}_{\cal H})\varphi\rangle-e^{\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}/2}|&\leq|\langle\varphi|{\cal T}_{X,Y,n}^{n}(\mathbf{1}_{\cal H}-x_{X,Y,n})\varphi\rangle|\quad(I)\\
&+|\lambda_{X,Y,n}|^{n}|1-\langle\varphi|x_{X,Y,n}\varphi\rangle|\quad(II)\\
&+|\lambda_{X,Y,n}^{n}-e^{\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}/2}|\quad(III).\end{split}

Using that
{\cal T}_{X,Y,n}
is a contraction (therefore
|\lambda_{X,Y,n}|\leq 1
as well), one gets that
(I)
and
(II)
are
O(n^{2(\delta-1/2)})
. Regarding
(III)
, one has that

|\lambda_{X,Y,n}-e^{\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}}|=\left|\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{X,Y,n}^{n-k}(\lambda_{X,Y,n}-e^{\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}/2n})e^{\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}(k-1)/n}\right|=O(n^{3\delta-1/2}),

where we used that
|\lambda_{X,Y,n}|,|e^{\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}}|\leq 1
and that

\lambda_{X,Y,n}-e^{\ddot{\lambda}_{X,Y}/2n}=O(n^{3(\delta-1/2)}).

Putting everything together, we got that

\sup_{X,Y\in B_{r_{n}}^{\rm ext}(V_{0})}|\langle\Psi_{V(Xn^{-1/2})}|\Psi_{V(Yn^{-1/2})}\rangle-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{V_{0}}(X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id},X^{\rm id}-Y^{\rm id})+i\sigma_{V_{0}}(X^{\rm id},Y^{\rm id})}|=O(n^{3\delta-1/2}).

Therefore we can apply Lemma
2
where
r_{n}=n^{\delta}
,
\alpha=2
and
f(n)=O(n^{3\delta-1/2})
. Let us choose
\delta_{n}
of the form
n^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0.
The Le Cam distance between the two statistical models scales as

n^{-2\beta}+n^{3\delta/2-1/4}+n^{(3+d_{\rm id})\delta/4+d_{\rm id}\beta/4-1/8},

therefore it converges to
0
if
\delta<(2(3+d_{\rm id}))^{-1}
and
\beta<1/2{d_{\rm id}}-(3+d_{\rm id})\delta/d_{\rm id}.
If we want the distance above to decay faster than
n^{-2\delta}
we need to impose that

\delta<\min\{1/14,(2(11+d_{\rm id}))^{-1}\},\quad\delta<\beta<1/2d_{\rm id}-(11+d_{\rm id})\delta/d_{\rm id}.

Notice that
[\delta<\beta<1/2d_{\rm id}-(11+d_{\rm id})\delta/d_{\rm id}]
is bigger than a single point if
\delta<(2(11+d_{\rm id}))^{-1}.

∎

For the sake of conciseness and since it does not generate any confusion, we drop the label
V_{0}
from all the objects corresponding to such parameter, putting the emphasis on the local parameters. Let us consider a spectral resolution of
\rho^{\rm ss}=\rho^{\rm ss}_{V_{0}}
:

\rho^{\rm ss}=\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}\sum_{i\in I_{a}}\pi_{i}^{a}\ket{\phi_i^a}\bra{\phi_i^a}.

We recall that
\{\ket{\phi_i^a}\}_{i\in I_{a}}
is an orthonormal basis for the range of
P_{a}
. We recall that the stationary output can be expressed as

\rho^{\rm out}_{X}(n):=\rho^{\rm out}_{[V(Xn^{-1/2})]}(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b=0,\dots,p-1\\
i\in I_{a},j\in I_{b}\end{subarray}}\pi_{i}^{a}|\psi_{ij,X}^{ab}(n)\rangle\langle\psi_{ij,X}^{ab}(n)|,

(91)

where
|\psi^{ab}_{ij,X}(n)\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in I^{(n)}}\langle\phi_{j}^{b}|\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{i},X}^{(n)}|\phi_{i}^{a}\rangle\otimes\ket{\mathbf{i}}
.

In the primitive case (
p=1
), the output QLAN result is based on the fact that
|\psi_{ij,X}^{00}(n)\rangle
are quasi-orthogonal to each other for local parameters, so one only needs to prove QLAN for each component.
Consider an initial state in
\mathcal{H}_{a}
. Then after
n
evolution steps for
X=0
, the system state belongs to the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{a\oplus n}
(where
\oplus
denotes addition modulo
p
). Therefore

|\psi^{ab}_{ij}(n)\rangle=0,\qquad{\rm for~}b\neq n\oplus a.

This suggests that in order to have well defined limits,
one needs to consider subsequences of the type
n=p\cdot l+r
where
r
is fixed and
l\in\mathbb{N}
is allowed to grow
.

We now let
X,Y\in B_{r_{n}}^{\rm id}(0)
be arbitrary fixed local parameters and we study the limit for
l\rightarrow\infty
of the inner products

\langle\psi^{ab}_{ij,X}(n)|\psi^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime},Y}(n)\rangle=\left.\left.{\rm Tr}\left(\left|\phi_{i^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}}\right\rangle\right\langle\phi_{i}^{a}\right|{\cal T}^{n}_{X,Y,n}\left(\ket{\phi_{j}^b}\bra{\phi_{j^\prime}^{b^\prime}}\right)\right).

where
{\cal T}^{n}_{X,Y,n}
is the deformed channel which has been defined in the proof of Theorem
11
.

Lemma 12
.

Let us consider the following eigenvectors of the stationary state
\ket{\phi_{i}^a}
,
\ket{\phi_{i^\prime}^{a^\prime}}
,
\ket{\phi_{j}^b}
,
\ket{\phi_{ j^\prime}^{b^\prime}}
and the local parameters
X
,
Y
. One has

\sup_{X,Y\in B_{C(pl+r)^{\delta}}(0)}|\langle\psi^{ab}_{ij,X}(pl+r)|\psi^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime},Y}(pl+r)\rangle|=O(l^{\delta-1/2}\log(l))

unless
a=a^{\prime}
,
b=b^{\prime}
,
i=i^{\prime}
and
j=j^{\prime}
, in which case

\sup_{{X,Y\in B_{C(pl+r)^{\delta}}(0)}}|\langle\psi^{ab}_{ij,X}(pl+r)|\psi^{ab}_{ij,Y}(pl+r)\rangle-\pi_{j}^{b}p{\rm Tr}(\rho_{a}^{\rm ss}{\cal T}^{pl}_{X,Y,n}(P_{b\ominus r}))|=O(l^{\delta-1/2}\log(l)).

Let
A=\ket{\phi_{j}^b}\bra{\phi_{ j^\prime}^{b^\prime}}
,
A_{\infty}:=\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\pi_{j}^{b}pP_{b\ominus r}
,
we have

\displaystyle\|{\cal T}^{pl+r}_{X,Y,n}(A)-{\cal T}^{pl}_{X,Y,n}(A_{\infty})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|{\cal T}^{p(l-k)}_{X,Y,n}{\cal T}^{pk+r}_{X,Y,n}(A)-{\cal T}^{p(l-k)}_{X,Y,n}{\cal T}^{pk+r}(A)\|+\|{\cal T}^{p(l-k)}_{X,Y,n}{\cal T}^{pk+r}(A)-{\cal T}^{p(l-k)}_{X,Y,n}{\cal T}^{pk}_{X,Y,n}(A_{\infty})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|{\cal T}^{pk+r}_{X,Y,pl+r}(A)-{\cal T}^{pk+r}(A)\|+\|{\cal T}^{r}{\cal T}^{pk}(A)-{\cal T}^{pk}_{X,Y,n}(A_{\infty})\|,

\displaystyle\leq\|{\cal T}^{pk+r}_{X,Y,pl+r}(A)-{\cal T}^{pk+r}(A)\|+\|{\cal T}^{pk+r}(A)-A_{\infty}\|+\|{\cal T}^{pk}_{X,Y,pl+r}(A_{\infty})-A_{\infty}\|

where we used that
{\cal T}^{p}_{u,v,n}
is a contraction. Since
{\cal T}
and
{\cal T}_{X,Y,pl+r}
are contractions, we have

\|{\cal T}^{pk+r}_{X,Y,pl+r}(A)-{\cal T}^{pk+r}(A)\|\leq(pk+r)\|{\cal T}-{\cal T}_{X,Y,pl+r}\|=O(kl^{\delta-{1/2}})

which can be controlled if
k
increases sufficiently slowly. Next, we recall that

\mathcal{E}(A)=p\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}_{a}A)P_{a}=\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}p\pi^{b}_{j}P_{b}

so that
\mathcal{T}^{r}(\mathcal{E}(A)))=A_{\infty}
, and from (
25
) it follows that

\|{\cal T}^{r}{\cal T}^{pk}(A)-A_{\infty}\|=\|{\cal T}^{r}{\cal T}^{pk}(A)-{\cal T}^{r}(\mathcal{E}(A))\|\leq\|{\cal T}^{pk}-\mathcal{E}\|\rightarrow 0

exponentially fast in
k
. Moreover

\|{\cal T}^{pk}_{X,Y,pl+r}(A_{\infty})-A_{\infty}\|=\|{\cal T}^{pk}_{X,Y,pl+r}(A_{\infty})-{\cal T}^{pk}(A_{\infty})\|\leq pk\|{\cal T}-{\cal T}_{X,Y,pl+r}\|=O(kl^{-{1/2}+\delta}).

This proves that the limit of the inner product is zero if either
b\neq b^{\prime}
or
j\neq j^{\prime}
and that we can substitute
P_{b\ominus r}
to
{\cal T}^{r}_{X,Y,n}(A)
when computing the limit. Moving the map
{\cal T}_{X,Y,n}
on
\ket{\phi^{a^\prime}_{ i^\prime}}\bra{\phi_{i}^a}
by duality and repeating the same computations, we can conclude by choosing
k
increasing as
\log l
.

∎

Recall that the spectral projections of
Z
can be expressed as

P_{a}=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\overline{\gamma}^{ak}Z^{k}.

In order to compute the limit of
{\cal T}^{pl}_{X,Y,n}(P_{b\ominus r})
when
l\rightarrow\infty
, we can therefore study the limit of the powers of the same map applied to
Z^{k}
for
k=0,\dots,p-1
.

First, we need to introduce some some notation. Given any
p
-dimensional vectors
h
,
g
we denote in the following way the Fourier transform, the reflection around
0
and the convolution:

\hat{h}_{k}=\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\gamma^{mk}h_{m},\quad\widetilde{h}_{k}=h_{-k},\quad(h*g)_{k}=\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}h_{m}g_{k-m}.

We use
h^{*n}
to denote the convolution of
h
with itself
n
times (
(h^{*0})_{m}=\delta_{0,m}
) and we recall that
\widehat{h^{*n}}=\hat{h}^{n}
,
\widehat{h^{n}}=\widehat{h}^{*n}
and
\hat{\hat{h}}=\widetilde{h}
.

We also recall that
T^{\rm id}
decomposes as the direct sum

\bigoplus_{m=0}^{p-1}\mathcal{V}_{m}
where
\mathcal{V}_{m}
are the orthogonal eigenspaces of the unitary operator
d\mu^{g_{1}}_{V_{0}}
, cf. equation (
58
).
For
A\in T^{\rm id}
we denote its projection onto

\mathcal{V}_{m}
by
A_{m}
. Let us introduce the sesquilinear mapping
\eta:T^{\rm id}\times T^{\rm id}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{p}
defined as:

\eta(A,B)_{0}:=0,\quad\eta(A,B)_{m}={\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}A_{m}^{*}B_{m})=\beta(A_{m},B_{m})+i\sigma(A_{m},B_{m}).

Lemma 13
.

Let
k=1,\dots,p-1
. Then the following holds true:

\sup_{X,Y\in B_{C(pl+r)^{\delta}}(0)}\left\|{\cal T}^{pl}_{X,Y,pl}(Z^{k})-e^{\lambda_{k}(X,Y)}Z^{k}\right\|_{\infty}=O(l^{3\delta-1/2})

where

\begin{split}\lambda_{k}(X,Y)&=-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{V_{0}}(X_{0}-Y_{0},X_{0}-Y_{0})+i\sigma_{V_{0}}(X_{0},Y_{0})+i\alpha_{X}-i\alpha_{Y}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{V_{0}}(X^{\perp},X^{\perp})+\beta_{V_{0}}(Y^{\perp},Y^{\perp}))+\widehat{\eta(X,Y)}_{k},\end{split}

where
\alpha_{X}
and
\alpha_{Y}
are two (irrelevant) phases depending on
X
and
Y
only, respectively.

In this proof we use the notation
\langle A|B\rangle_{\rm HS}={\rm Tr}(A^{*}B)
.
Since the peripheral eigevalues are isolated and algebraically simple, we can repeat the perturbation argument in Theorem
11
to show that for every
k=0,\dots,p-1

\sup_{X,Y\in B_{Cn^{\delta}}(0)}\|{\cal T}^{n}_{X,Y,n}(Z^{k})-\gamma^{nk}e^{\gamma^{-k}\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(X,Y)}Z^{k}\|=O(n^{3\delta-1/2}),

where
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(X,Y)
has corresponding coefficient
\langle J_{k}|{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}(Z^{k})\rangle_{\rm HS}/2
.
We refer to the proof of Theorem
11
for the definition of
{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}
and
{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}
. Since the proof is the same, we will only report below the computations which are different.
By perturbation theory one has the following expansions for the perturbations of
\gamma^{k}
and
Z^{k}
:

\displaystyle\gamma^{-k}\gamma^{(k)}_{X,Y,n}=1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\gamma^{-k}\dot{\gamma}^{(k)}_{X,Y}+\frac{1}{2n}\gamma^{-k}\ddot{\gamma}^{(k)}_{X,Y}+O(n^{3(\delta-1/2)}),

\displaystyle Z^{(k)}_{X,Y,n}=Z^{k}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\dot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y}+\frac{1}{2n}\ddot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y}+O(n^{3(\delta-1/2)}),

where we assume without loss of generality that

\langle J_{k}|{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y}\rangle_{\rm HS}\equiv 1
. By differentiting the latter we get

\langle J_{k}|\dot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y}\rangle_{\rm HS}=0
.
On the other hand, differentiating the eigenvector equation, we obtain

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(Z^{k})+{\cal T}(\dot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y})

\displaystyle=\dot{\gamma}^{(k)}_{X,Y}Z^{k}+\gamma^{k}\dot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y},

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}(Z^{k})+2{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(\dot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y})+{\cal T}(\ddot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y})

\displaystyle=\ddot{\gamma}^{(k)}_{X,Y}Z^{k}+2\dot{\gamma}^{(k)}_{X,Y}\dot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y}+\gamma^{k}\ddot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y}.

Since
Z^{k}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V=\gamma^{k}VZ^{k}
we obtain

{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(Z^{k})=-iX^{*}Z^{k}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V_{0}+iV_{0}^{*}Z^{k}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y=\gamma^{k}(-iX^{*}V_{0}Z^{k}+iZ^{k}V_{0}^{*}Y)=0,

where we have used that
X,Y\in T^{\rm id}
implies
V_{0}^{*}X=V_{0}^{*}Y=0
(cf. Proposition
4
). Tracing the first equation against
J_{k}
and using
\langle J_{k}|\dot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y}\rangle_{\rm HS}=\langle J_{k}|\mathcal{T}(\dot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y})\rangle_{\rm HS}=0
one obtains
\dot{\gamma}^{(k)}_{X,Y}=0
.
As a consequence

\dot{Z}^{(k)}_{X,Y}=(\gamma^{k}{\rm Id}-{\cal T})^{-1}{\cal T}_{X,Y,1}(Z^{k})=0

and

2\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(X,Y):=\ddot{\gamma}^{(k)}_{X,Y}=\langle J_{k},{\cal T}_{X,Y,2}(Z^{k})\rangle_{\rm HS}.

Using again that
Z^{k}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}V=\gamma^{k}VZ^{k}
and the fact that
J_{k}Z^{k}=\rho^{\rm ss}
, one can write

\begin{split}\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(X,Y)&=\frac{\gamma^{k}}{2}{\rm Tr}(\rho^{\rm ss}(\partial^{2}_{X}V^{*}(0)V_{0}+V_{0}^{*}\partial^{2}_{Y}V(0)))+\langle J_{k},X^{*}Z^{k}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\cal K}Y\rangle.\end{split}

The statement follows taking
\lambda_{k}(X,Y)=\overline{\gamma}^{k}\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(X,Y)
; the other expression of
\lambda_{k}
follows from plugging in the expression of
J_{k}
and
Z^{k}
in terms of
P_{a}
’s.
∎

Putting together the results of Lemma
12
and Lemma
13
we obtain that

\sup_{X,Y\in B_{C(pl+r)^{\delta}}(0)}\left|\langle\psi_{ij,X}^{ab}(pl+r)|\psi_{ij,Y}^{ab}(pl+r)\rangle-\pi_{j}^{b}p\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\gamma^{(a-b+r)k}e^{\lambda_{k}(X,Y)}\right|=O(l^{3\delta-1/2}).

(92)

Recall that in section
5.1
we introduced the mixed Gaussian model
{\bf GM}_{V_{0}}
whose states can be expressed as mixtures according to equation (
63
).

\rho(X)=W(X_{0})\ket{\Omega_0}\bra{\Omega_0}W(X_{0})^{*}\otimes\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\ket{\zeta_m(X^\perp)}\bra{\zeta_m({X^\perp})}

The inner products of the right-side components are computed in the following lemma.

Lemma 14
.

Let us consider
X,Y\in T^{\rm id}
and
m,m^{\prime}=0,\dots,p-1
. Then

\langle\zeta_{m}(X^{\perp})|\zeta_{m^{\prime}}(Y^{\perp})\rangle=\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}\cdot e^{-(\beta(X^{\perp},X^{\perp})+\beta(Y^{\perp},Y^{\perp}))/2}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\gamma^{-mk}e^{\widehat{\eta(X,Y)}_{k}}.

(93)

As a consequence

\langle W(X_{0})\Omega_{0}\otimes\zeta_{m}(X^{\perp})|W(Y_{0})\Omega_{0}\otimes\zeta_{m^{\prime}}(Y^{\perp})\rangle=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\gamma^{-mk}e^{\lambda_{k}(X,Y)}.

The fact that
\ket{\zeta_m(X^\perp)}\perp\ket{\zeta_{m^\prime}(Y^\perp)}
if
m\neq m^{\prime}
is an easy consequence of the fact that they arise from the projection of
W(\widetilde{X})\ket{\Omega_0^\perp}
into orthogonal subspaces (see Eq. (
64
)). On the other hand,

\begin{split}\langle\zeta_{m}(X^{\perp})|\zeta_{m}(Y^{\perp})\rangle&=e^{-(\beta(X^{\perp},X^{\perp})+\beta(Y^{\perp},Y^{\perp}))/2}\left(\delta_{0}(m)+\right.\\
&\left.+\sum_{l\geq 1}\frac{1}{l!}\sum_{m_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus m_{l}=-m}\widetilde{\eta(X,Y)}_{m_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{\eta(X,Y)}_{m_{l}}\right)\\
&=e^{-(\beta(X^{\perp},X^{\perp})+\beta(Y^{\perp},Y^{\perp}))/2}\left(\delta_{0}(m)+\sum_{l\geq 1}\frac{1}{l!}{\widetilde{\eta(X,Y)}}^{*l}_{-m}\right)\\
&=e^{-(\beta(X^{\perp},X^{\perp})+\beta(Y^{\perp},Y^{\perp}))/2}\left(\delta_{0}(m)+\sum_{l\geq 1}\frac{1}{l!}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\gamma^{-km}(\widehat{\eta(X,Y)})^{l}_{k}\right)=\\
&e^{-(\beta(X^{\perp},X^{\perp})+\beta(Y^{\perp},Y^{\perp}))/2}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\gamma^{-mk}e^{\widehat{\eta(X,Y)}_{k}}.\end{split}

∎

In the last part of the proof we use equations (
92
) and (
93
) to prove the convergence of the Le Cam distance between the stationary output model and the mixed Gaussian model. For this we consider the following additional families of
(un-normalised) vectors

\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}(pl+r):=\left\{\ket{\psi_{ij,X}^{ab}(pl+r)}~:~a,b=0,\dots,p-1,\,i\in I_{a},\,j\in I_{b},\,X\in B^{\rm id}_{C(pl+r)^{\delta}}\right\},

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bf G}(pl+r):=

\displaystyle\left\{|\varphi^{ab}_{ij,X}\rangle:=\sqrt{\pi_{j}^{b}p}\,W(X_{0})\ket{\Omega_0}\otimes\ket{\zeta_{b\ominus a \oplus r}(X^\perp)}\otimes\ket{a,b,i,j}~:~a,b=0,\dots,p-1,\right.

\displaystyle\left.i\in I_{a},\,j\in I_{b},\,X\in B^{\rm id}_{C(pl+r)^{\delta}}\right\}\subset{\cal F}(T^{\rm id}_{V_{0}})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{s},

where
s=2(p-1)+\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}|I_{a}|
(see section
5.1
for the definition of the involved objects).

Lemma
12
, equation (
92
) and Lemma
14
imply that for any fixed
r=0,\dots,p-1
, the inner products of the vectors in

\widetilde{\bf Q}(pl+r)
converge uniformly to the inner products of the vectors in

\widetilde{\bf G}(pl+r)
with a rate of the order
l^{3\delta-1/2}
.

Using that

\ket{W(X_0)\Omega_0}\otimes\ket{\zeta_{b\ominus a \oplus r}(X^\perp)}=\mathbf{1}\otimes Q_{-b\ominus a\oplus r}W(X)\ket{\Omega},

one has that the distance in norm
1
between the states (as density matrices)

\ket{W(X_0)\Omega_0}\otimes\ket{\zeta_{b\ominus a \oplus r}(X^\perp)}\otimes\ket{a,b,i,j}

and

\ket{W(Y_0)\Omega_0}\otimes\ket{\zeta_{a\ominus b \ominus r}(Y^\perp)}\otimes\ket{a,b,i,j}

is less or equal that

\|\ket{W(X)\Omega}\bra{W(X)\Omega}-\ket{W(Y)\Omega}\bra{W(Y)\Omega}\|_{1}\leq\beta_{V_{0}}(X-Y,X-Y).

Therefore one can apply Lemma
9
, one has that

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\Delta(\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}(pl+r),\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}(pl+r))=0.

(94)

We arrived at the last step of the proof.
Consider the stationary output model (with
n=pl+r
)

\mathbf{Q}^{\rm out}_{V_{0}}(pl+r):=\{\rho_{[V(X(pl+r)^{-1/2})]}^{\rm out}(pl+r)~:~X\in B^{\rm id}_{C(pl+r)^{\delta}}(V_{0})\}

and the modified Gaussian mixture model

\widetilde{\mathbf{GM}}(pl+r):=\left\{\omega_{X}:=\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}W(X_{0})\ket{\Omega_0}\bra{\Omega_0}W(X_{0})^{*}\otimes\ket{\zeta_m(X^\perp)}\bra{\zeta_m(X^\perp)}\otimes\omega_{m}\right\},

(95)

where

\omega_{m}:=\sum_{a,i,j}\pi_{i}^{a}\pi_{j}^{a\ominus m\ominus r}p\ket{a,a \ominus m\ominus r,i,j}\bra{a,a \ominus m\ominus r,i,j}.

is a state for every
X\in B^{\rm id}_{C(pl+r)^{\delta}}(V_{0})
:

{\rm Tr}(\omega_{m})=p\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}\left(\sum_{i}\pi_{i}^{a}\right)\left(\sum_{j}\pi_{j}^{a\ominus m\ominus r}\right)=p\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}\frac{1}{p^{2}}=1.

Let us now show that equation (
67
) in the theorem statement is a corollary of (
94
).

Notice that

\displaystyle\rho^{\rm out}_{X}(n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,i,j}\ket{\sqrt{\pi}_i^a \psi^{ab}_{ij,X}(n)}\bra{\sqrt{\pi}_i^a \psi^{ab}_{ij,X}(n)},

\displaystyle\omega_{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,i,j}\ket{\varphi^{ab}_{ij,X}}\bra{\varphi^{ab}_{ij,X}},

therefore thanks to equation (
94
) and Lemma
3
we get that

\lim_{l\rightarrow+\infty}\Delta(\mathbf{Q}^{\rm out}_{V_{0}}(pl+r),\widetilde{\mathbf{GM}}(pl+r))=0.

Equation (
67
) follows from the fact that
\mathbf{GM}(pl+r))
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{GM}}(pl+r))
are equivalent: indeed, let us consider the two quantum channels given by

\displaystyle{\cal S}_{*}:L^{1}({\cal F}(T^{\rm id}))\otimes L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{s})

\displaystyle\rightarrow L^{1}({\cal F}(T^{\rm id}))

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\mapsto{\rm Tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{s}}(\rho)

and

\displaystyle{\cal R}_{*}:L^{1}({\cal F}(T^{\rm id}))

\displaystyle\rightarrow L^{1}({\cal F}(T^{\rm id}))\otimes L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{s})

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}(\mathbf{1}\otimes Q_{m})\rho(\mathbf{1}\otimes Q_{m})\otimes\omega_{m}.

It is easy to see that for every
X\in T^{\rm id}
one has

{\cal S}_{*}(\omega_{X})=\rho(X)\text{ and }{\cal R}_{*}(\rho(X))=\omega_{X}.

The opposite direction can be shown by performing a similar construction.

∎

G
Proofs of Lemma
7

One can explicitly check that
{\cal T}_{0}(\cdot)-{\rm Tr}(\cdot)\mathbf{1}/2
is diagonalisable with the following eigenvalues and eigenvectors:

\displaystyle(\lambda_{1}(0)=0,R_{1}(0)=\mathbf{1}/2),\,(\lambda_{2}(0)=-1,R_{1}(0)=Z),

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{3}(0)=1/\sqrt{3}+1/\sqrt{6},R_{3}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{4}(0)=-(1/\sqrt{3}+1/\sqrt{6}),R_{4}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\right).

Perturbation theory ensures that we can define smooth functions
\lambda_{j}(\theta),L_{j}(\theta),R_{j}(\theta)
such that

1.

{\cal T}_{\theta}(R_{j}(\theta))=\lambda_{j}(\theta)R_{j}(\theta),

2.

{\cal T}_{\theta}^{\dagger}(L_{j})=\overline{\lambda_{j}(\theta)}L_{j}(\theta)
, where
{\cal T}_{\theta}^{\dagger}
is the adjoint of
{\cal T}_{\theta}
with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product,

3.

\langle L_{j}(\theta)|R_{j}(\theta)\rangle_{HS}\equiv 1
.

We recall that for every
\theta
,

{\cal T}_{0*}(\mathbf{1}/2)={\cal T}_{0}(\mathbf{1}/2)=\mathbf{1}/2,

moreover one can easily check that
{\cal T}_{\theta}(Z)=(-1+2\sin^{2}(\theta))Z
as well. Therefore, since
|\lambda_{1}(0)|,|\lambda_{3}(0)|,|\lambda_{4}(0)|<|\lambda_{2}(0)|=1,
the spectral radius of
{\cal T}_{\theta}-{\rm Tr}(\cdot)\mathbf{1}/2
is given by
|\lambda_{2}(\theta)|=1-2\sin^{2}(\theta)
and, consequently, the spectral radius of
\widetilde{{\cal T}}_{\theta}={\cal T}^{2}_{\theta}-{\rm Tr}(\cdot)\mathbf{1}/4
is
(1-2\sin^{2}(\theta))^{2}
. Moreover, one can easily see that for every
k\geq 0

({\cal T}_{\theta}-{\rm Tr}(\cdot)\mathbf{1}/2)^{k}={\cal V}_{\theta}^{-1}{\cal D}^{k}_{\theta}{\cal V}_{\theta},\quad\text{ where }{\cal D}_{\theta}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}(\theta)&0&0\\
0&0&\lambda_{3}(\theta)&0\\
0&0&0&\lambda_{4}(\theta)\\
\end{pmatrix},

and
{\cal V}_{\theta}
is the transformation that allows to pass from the basis composed by the right eigenvectors
R_{1}(\theta),\dots,R_{4}(\theta)
to the canonical basis, hence whose rows are composed by the conjugate of the coordinates of
L_{j}(\theta)
s in the canonical basis. We can easily see that

\|\widetilde{{\cal T}}^{k}_{\theta}\|_{2\rightarrow 2}=\|{\cal V}_{\theta}^{-1}{\cal D}^{2k}_{\theta}{\cal V}_{\theta}\|_{2\rightarrow 2}\leq\|{\cal V}_{\theta}^{-1}\|_{2\rightarrow 2}\|{\cal V}_{\theta}\|_{2\rightarrow 2}\|{\cal D}^{2k}_{\theta}\|_{2\rightarrow 2}\leq C|\lambda_{2}(\theta)|^{2k},

where

C:=\max_{\theta\in[0,\overline{\theta}]}\|{\cal V}_{\theta}^{-1}\|_{2\rightarrow 2}\|{\cal V}_{\theta}\|_{2\rightarrow 2}>0.

The maximum in the previous equation is well defined and strictly positive due to the continuity of the norm and the compactness of the interval.
∎
